Boundary Harnack estimates of optimal order for kinetic Fokker-Planck equations
Kyeongbae Kim, Marvin Weidner

Abstract
. We establish higher order boundary Harnack estimates for solutions to kinetic Fokker-Planck equations with absorbing incoming boundaries. Unlike classical elliptic and parabolic equations with Dirichlet data, we show that the quotient of two solutions for kinetic equations is not C ∞ C^{\infty} up to the boundary. Instead, we develop a general theory showing that, near the grazing set, the quotient of two solutions is C 3 / 2 C^{3/2} if the domain and data are sufficiently smooth, and C 1 , 1 C^{1,1} in the absence of source terms. These exponents are optimal.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q84, 35B65, 82C40

1.
Introduction

The goal of this article is to establish higher order boundary Harnack-type estimates for solutions to kinetic Fokker-Planck equations

(1.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F\quad\text{for }(t,v,x)\in(-1,0]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

subject to absorbing incoming boundaries, namely

(1.2)

\displaystyle f=0\quad\text{in }(-1,0]\times\gamma_{-},

where
\gamma_{\pm}:=(-1,0]\times\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\pm v\cdot n_{x}>0\}
.
This class of equations plays a central role in kinetic theory where it arises naturally as the linearization of the Landau equation from plasma physics
[
11
,
26
,
29
,
31
,
18
,
25
,
19
]
. Moreover, it models Langevin dynamics
[
40
,
46
,
34
,
23
,
51
]
and is connected to the pricing of Asian options in mathematical finance
[
20
,
4
,
9
]
.

From a purely mathematical perspective, (
1.1
) is of high interest due to the hypoellipticity of the governing degenerate operator
f\mapsto(v\cdot\nabla_{x}-\Delta_{v})f
, an effect that was first described by Kolmogorov
[
39
]
.
Due to its relevance in all of these fields, the regularity theory for kinetic Fokker-Planck equations has been an intensely active research area over the past two decades
[
49
,
50
,
22
,
2
,
37
,
32
,
31
,
33
,
24
,
45
,
57
,
53
]
.

The starting point of this article is the striking phenomenon that the hypoelliptic smoothing of (
1.1
) deteriorates at the boundary of the solution domain. Specifically, near the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
, where velocities are tangent to the boundary,

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}:=(-1,0]\times\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:v\cdot n_{x}=0\}

the kinetic transport operator degenerates and therefore, the structure of the equation no longer generates full kinetic regularity in all variables. For sufficiently smooth coefficients and domains, solutions of (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) are known to be
C^{1/2}
near
\gamma_{0}
(see
[
30
,
60
,
38
]
), however they generally fail to be more regular as is indicated by explicit counterexamples
\phi_{0}\in C^{1/2}
(see (
1.5
)).

This loss of regularity raises the natural question whether the
C^{1/2}
regularity is a particular feature of certain special solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) or if the interplay between the geometry at
\gamma_{0}
and the kinetic equation affects all solutions similarly. More generally, it is natural to ask:

Do all solutions to (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) exhibit the same boundary behavior near the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
?

The goal of this article is precisely to answer this question. To measure the similarity of the boundary behavior of two solutions
f_{1},f_{2}
to (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) we establish a higher order boundary Harnack estimate which amounts to analyzing the boundary regularity of the quotient
f_{1}/f_{2}
. This is a natural approach, since if both solutions exhibit the same leading-order singular boundary behavior near
\gamma_{0}
, then that singularity should cancel, making
f_{1}/f_{2}
more regular than
C^{1/2}
.

1.1.
Main result

In
Theorem 1.1
we establish a
complete characterization of the uniform boundary behavior
of solutions to (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) by proving optimal regularity estimates of their quotient
f_{1}/f_{2}
. Our result seems to be the first kinetic boundary Harnack estimate at spatial boundaries. We show that the regularity of
f_{1}/f_{2}
depends heavily on the direction from which
\gamma_{0}
is approached. To understand this crucial aspect of our result, we decompose the interior of the solutions domain into three regions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{+}\coloneqq\{d_{\Omega}(x)\leq(n_{x}\cdot v)^{3}\},\quad\mathcal{R}_{-}\coloneqq\{d_{\Omega}(x)\leq-(n_{x}\cdot v)^{3}\},\quad\mathcal{R}_{0}\coloneqq\{d_{\Omega}(x)\geq|n_{x}\cdot v|^{3}\}.

Here,
d_{\Omega}(x):=\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
and
n_{x}
stands for the outer unit normal vector at the projection
\bar{x}\in\partial\Omega
of
x
to
\partial\Omega
. Note that near the boundary
\partial\Omega
the regions
\mathcal{R}_{+}
and
\mathcal{R}_{-}
naturally extend the outgoing and incoming boundaries
\gamma_{\pm}
into the domain and can hence be seen as the outgoing and incoming boundary layers, respectively. In contrast,
\mathcal{R}_{0}
only contains velocities that are nearly tangent to
\partial\Omega
and therefore represents the grazing region
1
1

1

Geometrically, projecting a phase-space point
(x,v)
to the kinetic boundary
\gamma=\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
via a suitably scaled kinetic distance
d_{\Omega}(x)+|v\cdot n_{x}|^{3}
maps
\mathcal{R}_{+}
,
\mathcal{R}_{-}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{0}
exactly to
\gamma_{+}
,
\gamma_{-}
, and
\gamma_{0}
, respectively.
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth domain with
0\in\partial\Omega
. Let
f_{1},f_{2}
be solutions to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f_{i}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{i}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f_{i})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{i}+F_{i}&&\qquad\mbox{in $Q_{2}\cap\big((-4,0]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)=:H_{2}$},\\
f_{i}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap Q_{2}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

with
f_{2},F_{2}\geq 0
and
A\geq\Lambda^{-1}I
. Then, there is
\rho_{1}=\rho_{1}(\Lambda,\Omega)>0
such that if
f_{2}(-1,-\rho_{1}^{3}n_{0},0)=c_{0}>0
for some
c_{0}>0
, then the following hold true:

(i)

There is
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,\Omega)
such that if for some
\epsilon>0
,
A\in C^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(H_{2})
,
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{2})
, and
F_{1},F_{2}\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})
with

(1.3)

\displaystyle\|F_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{\mathcal{M}},

then

\displaystyle\left[\frac{f_{1}}{f_{2}}\right]_{C^{3/2}(H_{\frac{\rho_{1}}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq c\sum_{i=1}^{2}\big(\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}+\|F_{i}\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})}\big),

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,c_{0},\|A\|_{C^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})},\Omega)
.

•

If
F_{1}\equiv F_{2}\equiv 0
and for some
\epsilon>0
,
A\in C^{1+\epsilon}(H_{2})
,
B\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})
, then

\displaystyle\left[\frac{f_{1}}{f_{2}}\right]_{C^{1,1}(H_{\frac{\rho_{1}}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq c\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,c_{0},\|A\|_{C^{1+\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})},\Omega)
.

Our result indicates that solutions exhibit a universal leading-order boundary profile near the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
within
\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}_{+}
. Consequently, taking the quotient of two solutions factors out their dominant singularities, yielding regularity up to order
C^{3/2}
(or
C^{1,1}
if
F_{i}\equiv 0
). We show in
subsection 5.5
that the exponents
C^{3/2}
in (i) and
C^{1,1}
in (ii) are optimal, even for the Kolmogorov equation in the half-space. This proves that the universal boundary behavior does not extend to higher-order asymptotic terms, which remain singular and are highly solution-dependent.

We emphasize that
Theorem 1.1
does not hold for the incoming boundary layer
\mathcal{R}_{-}
, where the quotient
f_{1}/f_{2}
is generally
unbounded
, even away from
\gamma_{0}
. In
subsection 5.6
we provide explicit examples of nonnegative solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) that remain smooth up to
\gamma_{-}
, but whose quotient is unbounded
2
2

2

We can show higher regularity of
f_{1}/f_{2}
as in
Theorem 1.1
outside the smaller set
\mathcal{R}_{-}^{M}=\{Md_{\Omega}(x)\leq-(n_{x}\cdot v)^{3})\}
for any
M>1
. In that case, the constants depend on
M
and explode as
M\to\infty
. See also the discussion in
subsection 5.5
.
. This failure of the boundary Harnack principle can be attributed to the infinite order decay of solutions near
\gamma_{-}
(see
[
54
,
60
,
38
]
).

Inside the outgoing boundary layer
\mathcal{R}_{+}
, but away from
\gamma_{0}
, we can prove that the quotient
f_{1}/f_{2}
is actually smooth (see
subsection 5.6
). This is due to the fact that solutions are smooth up to
\gamma_{+}
(see
[
53
]
) but do not vanish at
\gamma_{+}
, as no boundary condition can be prescribed there.

Ultimately, we emphasize that
Theorem 1.1
stands in stark contrast to classical higher order boundary Harnack estimates for elliptic and parabolic equations (see
[
16
,
6
,
41
,
55
,
58
,
35
]
), where quotients of solutions always remain
C^{\infty}
. Remarkably, boundary Harnack estimates of any order even hold for nonlocal problems (see
[
1
]
), where solutions are in general only Hölder continuous up to the boundary. In this light, our result reveals a distinguishing feature of kinetic equations: the degeneracy of the hypoelliptic effect at the grazing set governs not only the absolute behavior of solutions, but their relative behavior as well. To our knowledge,
Theorem 1.1
is the first result to capture this phenomenon.

We make several further comments on our result.

Remark 1.2
.

(i)

In light of higher order boundary Harnack estimates for elliptic equations (see
[
55
]
), our assumptions on the coefficients
A,B
seem to be sharp within the class of Hölder spaces.

(ii)

In
Theorem 5.10
we prove boundary Harnack estimates of order
\alpha\in(0,3/2]
under weaker assumptions on
A,B,F_{i},\Omega
, and of order
\alpha\in(3/2,2]
in case
F_{i}=0
. This is the most general version of our main result and it can be seen as a Schauder-type theory for
f_{1}/f_{2}
.

(iii)

The smallness of
F_{2}
in (
1.3
) is a natural assumption in the literature (see for instance
[
56
]
) and is required in order to propagate the positivity of
f_{2}
from
(-1,-\rho_{1}^{3}n_{0},0)
to
H_{\rho_{1}/8}
via Harnack chains (see
section 3
). It is possible to drop the assumption (
1.3
) if we impose that

\displaystyle\inf_{H_{c_{0}}(-1,-\rho_{1}^{3}n_{0},0)}f_{2}\geq c_{0},

which allows to propagate positivity of
f_{2}
as in
section 3
using the weak Harnack inequality.

(iv)

Note that
Theorem 1.1
yields regularity only in a
\rho_{1}
-neighborhood of the boundary. It seems that this neighborhood can in general not be enlarged since the propagation of positivity is distorted by the kinetic transport operator (see the discussion in Section
3
).

(v)

We emphasize that our main result holds true for solutions in kinetic cylinders
Q_{1}
that are one-sided in time (see Section
2
for the precise definition).

Remark 1.3
.

In
[
38
, Theorem 1.7]
, we have established that
f/\Phi\in C^{0,1}(\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}_{+})
for an explicitly constructed profile
\Phi\in C^{1/2}
. While this result characterizes the boundary behavior of an individual solution
f
, the fact that
\Phi
is not a solution
limits the regularity of the quotient
f/\Phi
. Indeed, the
C^{0,1}
regularity in
[
38
, Theorem 1.7]
is optimal (see
section 6
). In contrast, the higher order estimates in
C^{3/2}
and
C^{1,1}
obtained in
Theorem 1.1
demonstrate that boundary Harnack estimates are essential for capturing the higher order solution behavior. Moreover, note that, as we explain below, the proof of
Theorem 1.1
differs fundamentally from the one of
[
38
, Theorem 1.7]
. Most importantly, the proof of
Theorem 1.1
not only hinges on fine boundary expansions, but also on lower solutions bounds such as in
Theorem 1.5
.

1.2.
Discussion of the literature

Boundary Harnack principles have a rich history due to their various applications in potential theory
[
36
,
15
]
, free boundary problems
[
10
,
5
]
,
[
3
,
16
,
17
,
52
,
58
]
, and probability theory
[
7
,
8
]
. Classical results establish that quotients of elliptic or parabolic solutions are Hölder continuous up to the boundary, even in rough domain (Lipschitz or worse). Our main result belongs to the distinct class of higher order boundary Harnack estimates, which provide a Schauder-type regularity theory for quotients of two solutions and have been established in
[
16
,
6
,
41
,
55
,
58
,
35
]
for elliptic and parabolic equations.

For kinetic equations,
Theorem 1.1
provides the first regularity estimate for quotients of solutions near spatial boundaries. Previous results
[
13
,
14
]
have established Carleson-type estimates near Lipschitz boundaries, but even the Hölder regularity of
f_{1}/f_{2}
has remained open. On the other hand, let us mention the seminal series of articles
[
47
,
48
,
43
,
42
,
44
]
, where the authors establish fine properties of the kinetic harmonic measure, as well as comparison principles, and boundary Harnack estimates of class
C^{\alpha}
. Crucially, however, their analysis is restricted to domains of the form

(1.4)

\displaystyle\{(t,x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+2n}:v_{n}>\psi(t,x,v^{\prime})\},\quad\text{where }\psi\text{ is Lipschitz continuous}.

The geometry of (
1.4
) is fundamentally different from the one of spatial domains as considered in
Theorem 1.1
. In fact, the boundary of (
1.4
) is everywhere
non-characteristic
in the sense that it is “seen” by the diffusion in
v
and therefore a boundary condition needs to be prescribed everywhere. This leads to a solution behavior resembling much more the one of elliptic and parabolic equations. Spatial boundaries, in contrast, are “invisible” to the diffusion in
v
and all interaction with the boundary is governed by the transport operator. The analysis of spatial domains requires different tools which we develop in this article.

Remark 1.4
.

In
[
47
, Remark 1.5]
, the authors ask whether boundary Harnack estimates can also hold in domains different from (
1.4
). In this paper, we provide a partial answer to this question for the class of spatial domains. It is an interesting research topic to investigate boundary Harnack estimates in spatial Lipschitz domains or in domains with boundaries jointly in
(t,x,v)
. The latter case could be relevant for the study of the free boundary in kinetic obstacle-type problems (see
[
9
]
).

1.3.
Strategy of the proof

The key difficulty in the proof of our main result arises from the singular solution behavior near the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
, which has recently been investigated in
[
38
]
(see also
[
51
,
28
,
60
]
). The main work in this article consists in establishing a Hopf-type lemma and novel fine expansions of solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) near the grazing set, as we explain below.

The central object in this analysis is the explicit solution
\phi_{0}

(1.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}\phi_{0}-\partial_{vv}\phi_{0}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x>0\}\times\mathbb{R}$},\\
\phi_{0}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x=0\}\times\{v>0\}$},\end{aligned}\right.

which was computed in
[
21
,
23
]
(see also
[
38
]
)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,v)=\begin{cases}x^{1/6}U\left(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{2}{3},-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)&\quad\text{if }v<0,\\
\frac{\Gamma(7/6)}{\Gamma(1/6)}x^{1/6}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}U\left(\frac{5}{6},\frac{2}{3},\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)&\quad\text{if }v\geq 0.\end{cases}

The function
\phi_{0}
has the following asymptotic boundary behavior (see
[
21
,
23
,
28
,
38
]
)

(1.6)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,v)\eqsim\begin{cases}x^{\frac{1}{6}}\left(\frac{v^{3}}{x}\right)^{-\frac{5}{6}}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}\quad&\text{in }\{x<v^{3}\}=\mathcal{R}_{-},\\
x^{\frac{1}{6}}\quad&\text{in }\{x\geq|v|^{3}\}=\mathcal{R}_{0},\\
|v|^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad&\text{in }\{x<-v^{3}\}=\mathcal{R}_{+},\end{cases}

and it is easy to see that
\phi_{0}\in C^{\frac{1}{2}}\setminus C^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}
and is homogeneous of degree
\frac{1}{2}
, i.e.,
\phi_{0}(\lambda^{3}x,\lambda v)=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{0}(x,v)
.

The first main ingredient in the proof of
Theorem 1.1
is the following lower bound for solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
). This lemma plays the same role as the classical Hopf lemma for elliptic or parabolic equations. To our knowledge, this is the first result of its kind for kinetic equations with spatial boundaries, and we believe it is of independent interest.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{2,\epsilon}
domain for some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
f\geq 0
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{2}$},\\
f&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{2}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
A\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})
with
A\geq\Lambda^{-1}I
,
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{2})
, and
0\leq F\in L^{\infty}(H_{2})
.

Then, there are
\rho_{1}=\rho_{1}(\Lambda,\Omega)>0
and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,\Omega)
such that if

\displaystyle f(-1,-\rho_{1}^{3}n_{0},0)=c_{0}>0,\qquad\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{\mathcal{M}},

for some
c_{0}>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{c}\leq\inf_{H_{\frac{\rho_{1}}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}}\frac{f}{\Phi}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})},\Omega)
, where
{\Phi(x,v)\coloneqq\phi_{0}(d_{\Omega}(x),v\cdot n_{x})}
.

The proof of the Hopf lemma proceeds in three main steps. First, we establish a propagation of positivity result for solutions to kinetic equations (see
section 3
). To do so, we employ suitable Harnack chains to propagate the pointwise information at
(-1,-\rho_{1}^{3}n_{0},0)
to points inside
H_{\rho_{1}/8}\cap{\{d_{\Omega}\geq(\rho_{1}/32)^{3}\}}
. This is quite delicate in the kinetic setting due to the specific geometry induced by the transport term and the fact that the waiting time in the kinetic Harnack inequality cannot be chosen arbitrarily small (see
[
12
]
).
In the second step we prove the Hopf lemma for equations with constant coefficients in the half-space by constructing suitable barrier functions (see
subsection 4.1
) and using the propagation of positivity result.
Finally, we develop a novel perturbation argument to generalize the Hopf lemma from constant to Hölder continuous coefficients (see Subsection
4.2
). This step is technically demanding since it relies on fine pointwise expansions of solutions at
\gamma_{0}
in terms of the highly anisotropic function
\phi_{0}
.

The second main ingredient in the proof of
Theorem 1.1
is to establish new expansions of solutions at
\gamma_{0}
in the half-space, i.e., when
\Omega=\{x_{n}>0\}
. In
[
38
, Proposition 5.1]
, we have already shown that any solution
f_{1}
to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) satisfies for any
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]

(1.7)

\displaystyle|f_{1}(z)-p_{0}(z)\phi_{0}(z)-p_{1}(z)\varphi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n})-b_{2}\varphi_{2}(x_{n},v_{n})-b_{3}\varphi_{3}(x_{n},v_{n})|\lesssim r^{3-\epsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall z\in H_{r},

where
p_{0},p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
b_{2},b_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
, and the functions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3}
are explicit solutions to

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}\varphi_{1}-\partial_{vv}\varphi_{1}&=\partial_{vv}\phi_{0},&\quad v\partial_{x}\varphi_{2}-\partial_{vv}\varphi_{2}&=1,&\quad v\partial_{x}\varphi_{3}-\partial_{vv}\varphi_{3}&=\phi_{0}&\qquad&\text{in }\{x>0\}\times\mathbb{R},\\
\varphi_{1}&=0,&\quad\varphi_{2}&=0,&\quad\varphi_{3}&=0&\qquad&\text{in }\{x=0\}\times\{v>0\}\end{aligned}\right.

which satisfy
\varphi_{1}(\lambda^{3}x,\lambda v)=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{1}(x,v)
,
\varphi_{2}(\lambda^{3}x,\lambda v)=\lambda^{2}\varphi_{2}(x,v)
,
\varphi_{3}(\lambda^{3}x,\lambda v)=\lambda^{2+\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{3}(x,v)
(see also Subsection
2.2
and Subsection
5.1
for a more detailed discussion).

The expansion (
1.7
) reveals the fine asymptotic behavior of solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) up to order three.
To prove a higher order boundary Harnack estimate, we need to significantly improve (
1.7
). The main idea is to transform (
1.7
) into an expansion for
f_{1}
in terms of another solution
f_{2}
, as follows:

(1.8)

\displaystyle|f_{1}(z)-q_{0}(z)f_{2}(z)-q_{1}(z)\varphi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n})-b^{\prime}_{2}\varphi_{2}(x_{n},v_{n})-b^{\prime}_{3}\varphi_{3}(x_{n},v_{n})|\lesssim r^{3-\epsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall z\in H_{r},

where
q_{0},q_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
b^{\prime}_{2},b^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
(see
subsection 5.2
). We establish (
1.8
) via a blow-up argument that is conceptually motivated by
[
1
]
. In the kinetic setting, however, the analysis is substantially more delicate due to the complexity of the error terms. Hence, the proof requires several new elements (see for instance the role of
subsection 5.1
). Another crucial feature of (
1.8
) is that we actually have to prove that
q_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
satisfies
q_{1}(0)=\nabla_{v}q_{1}(0)=0
which requires a very careful analysis.

Subsequently
Theorem 1.1
is deduced upon dividing (
1.8
) by
f_{2}
and transferring the regularity of the individual quotients

\displaystyle q_{0},\qquad q_{1}\frac{\varphi_{1}}{f_{2}},\qquad\frac{\varphi_{2}}{f_{2}},\qquad\frac{\varphi_{3}}{f_{2}}

to
f_{1}/f_{2}
. The key observation here is that the most singular function
\phi_{0}
from (
1.7
) has been eliminated in (
1.8
) while the singular behavior of
\varphi_{1}
near the origin is compensated by the conditions
q_{1}(0)=\nabla q_{1}(0)=0
. Precisely these two features are responsible for the higher regularity of the quotient
f_{1}/f_{2}
.

In light of the Hopf lemma in
Theorem 1.5
and the homogeneity of the functions
\varphi_{i}
, it becomes apparent that
q_{1}\frac{\varphi_{1}}{f_{2}}\asymp|z|^{2}
,
\frac{\varphi_{2}}{f_{2}}\asymp|z|^{\frac{3}{2}}
, and
\frac{\varphi_{3}}{f_{2}}\asymp|z|^{2}
as the kinetic distance
|z|=|x|^{\frac{1}{3}}+|v|\to 0
. This asymptotic behavior yields the
C^{3/2}
regularity of
f_{1}/f_{2}
established in
Theorem 1.1
(i). Moreover, we show that
\tilde{b}_{2}=0
in case
F_{i}\equiv 0
, which allows to improve the regularity of
f_{1}/f_{2}
to
C^{1,1}
in
Theorem 1.1
(ii).

1.4.
Outline

This paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
we introduce the kinetic Hölder spaces used in the article and recall several regularity results established in previous works. In Section
3
we establish a general propagation of positivity result for kinetic equations near spatial boundaries (see
section 3
). The Hopf lemma (see
Theorem 1.5
) is proved in Section
4
. Finally, Section
5
is dedicated to the proof of the higher order boundary Harnack estimate and establishes our main result
Theorem 1.1
. Moreover, we provide several counterexamples indicating the sharpness of our results in this section, as well as in the Appendix.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

Kyeongbae Kim and Marvin Weidner were supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG,
German Research Foundation) under Germany’s Excellence Strategy - EXC-2047/1 - 390685813 and through the CRC 1720 “Analysis of criticality: from complex phenomena to models and estimates”, 53930965.

2.
Preliminaries

Throughout the entire paper we always assume that for some constant
\Lambda\geq 1
, it holds

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}I\leq A\leq\Lambda I\quad\text{and}\quad|B|\leq\Lambda.

Next, let us collect some notation.

•

Let us write
X=(x,v)
. Given
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
X^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, where
X=(x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},v_{n})
.

•

Define a stationary cylinder by

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}_{r}\coloneqq B_{r^{3}}\times B_{r}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{H}_{r}\coloneqq B_{r^{3}}\times B_{r}\cap\{x_{n}>0\}.

In particular, we write
\mathrm{Q}^{\prime}_{r}\coloneqq B^{\prime}_{r^{3}}\times B^{\prime}_{r}
, where
B^{\prime}_{r}
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional ball with radius
r
.

•

Let us write
z=(t,x,v)
. Given
z\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
z^{\prime}=(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, where
z=(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},v_{n})
.

•

We set
I_{r^{2}}=(-r^{2},0]
. Define a kinetic cylinder by

\displaystyle Q_{r}\coloneqq I_{r^{2}}\times B_{r^{3}}\times B_{r}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{r}\coloneqq Q_{r}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Furthermore, we write
Q^{\prime}_{r}\coloneqq I_{r^{2}}\times B^{\prime}_{r^{3}}\times B^{\prime}_{r}
, where
B^{\prime}_{r}
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional ball with radius
r
.

•

For any
R>0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, we define
S_{R}z\coloneqq(R^{2}t,R^{3}x,Rv)
. For
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, we set

\displaystyle z_{0}\circ z=(t_{0}+t,x_{0}+x+tv_{0},v_{0}+v)

•

Given a domain
(a,b)\times\Omega\times V\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\Omega
and
V
being bounded sets, we define the kinetic boundary of
(a,b)\times\Omega\times V
by

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}((a,b)\times\Omega\times V)=(\{t=a\}\times\Omega\times V)\cup((a,b)\times\Omega\times\{v\in\partial V\})\cup\gamma_{-}.

•

For a given vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
v^{\mathrm{t}}
as a transpose of
v
, hence
v\cdot w=v^{\mathrm{t}}w
.

2.1.
Kinetic Hölder spaces

We recall the definition of the kinetic Hölder space and its properties.

First, we introduce a space of kinetic polynomials. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we define
\mathcal{P}_{k}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\left\{\sum_{|\beta|\leq k}t^{\beta_{t}}x^{\beta_{x_{1}}}\cdots x_{n}^{\beta_{n}}v_{1}^{\beta_{v_{1}}}\cdots v_{n}^{\beta_{v_{n}}}\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}\beta=(\beta_{t},\beta_{x_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{x_{n}},\beta_{v_{1}},\ldots,\beta_{v_{n}})\\
|\beta|=2|\beta_{t}|+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(3|\beta_{x_{i}}|+|\beta_{v_{i}}|)\end{array}\right\}.

Moreover, we write
\mathcal{P}_{-1}=\{0\}
.

Now, we define the kinetic Hölder semi-norm by

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k,\delta}(D)}\coloneqq\sup_{z_{0}\in D}\inf_{p_{z_{0}}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\sup_{z\in D}\frac{|(f-p_{z_{0}})(z)|}{\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{kin}}(z,z_{0})^{k+\delta}},

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\delta\in(0,1]
, where

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{kin}}(z,z_{0})\coloneqq\max\{|t-t_{0}|^{\frac{1}{2}},|x-(x_{0}+(t-t_{0})v_{0})|^{\frac{1}{3}},|v-v_{0}|\}.

In particular, we write for any
\delta\leq 0
,
[f]_{C^{\delta}(H_{R})}\coloneqq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}
.

Furthermore, we denote by
D^{k}
a
k
-th order differential operator. For example,
D
may represent
\partial_{v_{i}}
,
D^{2}
may denote operators such as
\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}
or
\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}
, and
D^{3}
can be
\partial_{v_{i},v_{j},v_{k}}
,
\partial_{v_{i}}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{v})
or
\partial_{x_{i}}
.

We now provide several auxiliary lemmas for kinetic Hölder spaces.

Lemma 2.1
.

[
53
, Lemma 2.10]
Let
r>0
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. Let
k,l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1]
. Suppose
k+\epsilon<l+\delta
. Then we have

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\leq c\left(r^{l+\delta-(k+\epsilon)}[f]_{C^{l,\delta}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{-(k+\epsilon)}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,k,l,\epsilon,\delta)
.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
33
, Lemma 2.7]
Let
r>0
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. Let
k,l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
. Then

\displaystyle[D^{l}f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\leq c[f]_{C^{k+l,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,k,l,\epsilon)
.

Next, we give the converse version of
subsection 2.1
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
r>0
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. Let
k\in\{1,2\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
. Then we have

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k+\epsilon}(Q_{r/2}(z_{0}))}\leq c\left([\nabla_{v}^{k}f]_{C^{\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}+\left[(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})^{\left[\frac{k+\epsilon}{2}\right]}f\right]_{C^{{k+\epsilon-2[(k+\epsilon)/2]}}_{\mathcal{T}}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}+[f]_{C^{{k+\epsilon}}_{x}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\right)

where we write for any
\alpha\in(0,2]
and
\beta\in(0,3]
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\coloneqq\sup_{z_{1},z=(t_{2},x_{1}+(t_{2}-t_{1})v_{1},v_{1})\in Q_{r}(z_{0})}\frac{|f(z_{1})-f(z)|}{\mathrm{dist}(z_{1},z)^{\alpha}},

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{\alpha}_{x}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\coloneqq\sup_{z_{1},z=(t_{1},x_{2},v_{1})\in Q_{r}(z_{0})}\frac{|f(z_{1})-f(z)|}{\mathrm{dist}(z_{1},z)^{\alpha}}.

A similar result can also be established for
k\geq 3
. Since it is not needed in this paper, we omit its statement and proof.

Proof.

We assume
r=2
and
z_{0}=0
. We observe from the definition of the Hölder space that

\displaystyle\sup_{z_{0}\neq z_{1}\in Q_{1}}\frac{|f(z_{1})-p_{z_{0},k}(z_{1})|}{\mathrm{dist}(z_{0},z_{1})^{k+\epsilon}}=[f]_{C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{1})},

where
p_{z_{0},k}
is the
k
-th order Taylor polynomial of
f
at
z_{0}
. In particular,
p_{z_{0},2}
is given by

\displaystyle p_{z_{0},2}(z)\coloneqq f(z_{0})+\nabla_{v}f(z_{0})\cdot(v-v_{0})+\frac{1}{2}(v-v_{0})^{\mathrm{t}}\nabla_{v}^{2}f(z_{0})(v-v_{0})+(\partial_{t}+v_{0}\cdot\nabla_{x})f(z_{0})(t-t_{0}).

Let us write

\displaystyle z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1},v_{1}),\quad\overline{z}_{1}\coloneqq(t_{0},x_{0},v_{1}),\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{z}_{1}\coloneqq(t_{1},x_{0}+(t_{1}-t_{0})v_{1},v_{1})

to see that
\overline{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{1}\in Q_{2}
and

(2.3)

\displaystyle\min\big\{\mathrm{dist}(\overline{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{1}),|v_{1}-v_{0}|,\mathrm{dist}(\widetilde{z}_{1},z_{1})\big\}\leq\mathrm{dist}(z_{0},z_{1}).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle|f(z_{1})-p_{z_{0},k}(z_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq|f(\overline{z}_{1})-p_{z_{0},k}(\overline{z}_{1})|+|f(\overline{z}_{1})-f(\widetilde{z}_{1})+(p_{z_{0},k}(\overline{z}_{1})-p_{z_{0},k}({z}_{1}))|+|f(z_{1})-f(\widetilde{z}_{1})|

\displaystyle\eqqcolon J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}.

For the two terms
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
, we use the fundamental theorem of calculus to see that

\displaystyle J_{1}\leq c[\nabla^{k}_{v}f]_{C^{\epsilon}(Q_{2})}|v_{1}-v_{0}|^{k+\epsilon},\quad J_{2}\leq c\left[(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})^{\left[\frac{k+\epsilon}{2}\right]}f\right]_{C^{{k+\epsilon-2[(k+\epsilon)/2]}}_{\mathcal{T}}(Q_{2})}\mathrm{dist}(\overline{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{1})^{{k+\epsilon-2[(k+\epsilon)/2]}}.

Next, by the definition of the Hölder space given in (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle J_{3}\leq c\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{z}_{1},z_{1})^{k+\epsilon}[f]_{C_{x}^{k+\epsilon}(Q_{2})}.

Thus, using the estimates
J_{1},J_{2}
and
J_{3}
together with (
2.3
), we derive

\displaystyle\frac{|f(z_{1})-p_{z_{0},k}(z_{1})|}{\mathrm{dist}(z_{0},z_{1})^{k+\epsilon}}\leq c\left([\nabla_{v}^{k}f]_{C^{\epsilon}(Q_{2})}+\left[(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})^{\left[\frac{k+\epsilon}{2}\right]}f\right]_{C^{{k+\epsilon-2[(k+\epsilon)/2]}}_{\mathcal{T}}(Q_{2})}+[f]_{C^{{k+\epsilon}}_{x}(Q_{2})}\right),

which gives

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k+\epsilon}(Q_{1})}\leq c\left([\nabla_{v}^{k}f]_{C^{\epsilon}(Q_{2})}+\left[(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})^{\left[\frac{k+\epsilon}{2}\right]}f\right]_{C^{{k+\epsilon-2[(k+\epsilon)/2]}}_{\mathcal{T}}(Q_{2})}+[f]_{C^{{k+\epsilon}}_{x}(Q_{2})}\right).

By this with
f
replaced by
f_{0}(z)\coloneqq f(z_{0}\circ S_{r/2}z)
, we have the desired estimate.
∎

2.2.
Regularity estimates

In this subsection, we collect several regularity estimates for kinetic equations (in the interior and up to the boundary) that have been established in previous works. In addition, we provide three explicit 1D solutions to Kolmogorov’s equation in the half space which appear in expansion estimates at the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
.

First, we recall interior Schauder type estimates of solutions to kinetic equations (see
[
53
, Lemma 2.23]
or
[
38
, Lemma 2.5]
).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
r>0
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
A\in C^{k-1,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))
,
B\in C^{k-2,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))
,
F\in C^{k-2,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))
and
G\in C^{k-1,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))
. For any weak solution
f
to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G)\quad\text{in }Q_{r}(z_{0}),

we have

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(Q_{r/2}(z_{0}))}\leq cr^{-(k+\epsilon)}\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{k+\epsilon}[F]_{C^{k-2,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{k+\epsilon}[G]_{C^{k-1,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{k-1,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))},\|B\|_{C^{k-2,\epsilon}(Q_{r}(z_{0}))})
.

Next, we provide the Hölder regularity of the solution at
\gamma_{+}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
r>0
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. Let
k\in\{1,2\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
A\in C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
,
B\in C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
,
F\in C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
and
G\in C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
with
x_{0,n}=0
,
v_{0,n}<0
and
H_{r}(z_{0})\cap\gamma_{0}=\emptyset
. Assume that
f
is a weak solution to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G)\quad\text{in }H_{r}(z_{0}).

Then we have

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))}\leq cr^{-(k+\epsilon)}\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{k+\epsilon}[F]_{C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{k+\epsilon}[G]_{C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))},\|B\|_{C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))})
.

Proof.

First note that the regularity at
\gamma_{+}
of kinetic equations in non-divergence form was established in
[
53
, Lemma 4.1]
by using a blow-up argument. The exact same argument also works for kinetic equations in divergence form as was explained in
[
38
, Lemma 6.1]
and also the term
\mathrm{div}_{v}G
can treated by the same approach (see
[
38
, Lemma 2.5]
). Note that all of these arguments also work for one-sided cylinders in
t
without any major modifications.
∎

Next, we recall the expansion estimates at the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
which were established in
[
38
]
. Here we argue that they remain true in one-sided cylinders.

Before stating the expansion estimates, we give three explicit solutions
\phi_{0}
,
\psi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
to Kolmogorov’s equation in the half-space in 1D, which were introduced and analyzed in
[
38
, Appendix C]
. As they appear in the expansion and they will also play an important role in Section
5
, we collect some of their basic properties below.

The functions
\phi_{0}=\phi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})
,
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})
and
\phi_{1}=\phi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n})
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}x_{n}^{1/6}U\left(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{2}{3},-\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}\right)&\quad\text{if }v_{n}<0,\\
\frac{\Gamma(7/6)}{\Gamma(1/6)}x_{n}^{1/6}e^{-\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}}U\left(\frac{5}{6},\frac{2}{3},\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}\right)&\quad\text{if }v_{n}\geq 0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}-x_{n}^{7/6}U\left(-\frac{7}{6},\frac{2}{3},-\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}\right)&\quad\text{if }v_{n}<0,\\
-\frac{\Gamma(13/6)}{\Gamma(-5/6)}x^{7/6}e^{-\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}}U\left(\frac{11}{6},\frac{2}{3},\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}\right)&\quad\text{if }v_{n}\geq 0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq x_{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(M\left(-\frac{2}{3},\frac{2}{3},-\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}\right)+\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\frac{2}{3}\right)}\frac{\Gamma\left(-\frac{1}{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)}\frac{v_{n}}{(9x_{n})^{1/3}}M\left(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{4}{3},-\frac{v_{n}^{3}}{9x_{n}}\right)\right)-av_{n}^{2},

for a suitable
a\in\mathbb{R}
(determined in
[
38
, Lemma C.6]
), where
U
denotes the Tricomi confluent hypergeometric function and
M
denotes a hypergeometric function of the first kind.

•

The function
\psi_{0}=\psi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})
solves

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}\psi_{0}-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}\psi_{0}&=1&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\\
\psi_{0}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

•

The function
\partial_{v}\phi_{1}=\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n})
solves

(2.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}(\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1})-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}(\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1})&=m_{0}\phi_{0}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\\
\partial_{v}\phi_{1}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$}\end{aligned}\right.

for some constant
m_{0}\neq 0
.

•

The function
v_{n}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{0}
solves

(2.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}(v_{n}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{0})-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}(v_{n}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{0})&=-3\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}\phi_{0}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\\
v_{n}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{0}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

•

We observe the homogeneity of the functions.

(2.7)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(S_{r}z)=r^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{0}(z),\quad\psi_{0}(S_{r}z)=r^{2}\psi_{0}(z),\quad\phi_{1}(S_{r}z)=r^{3+\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{1}(z).

•

Combining the regularity results given in
subsection 2.2
and
subsection 2.2
and the homogeneity as in (
2.7
), we derive for any
k\in\{0,1,2\}
and
\delta\in[0,1]
,

(2.8)

\displaystyle[\phi_{0}]_{C^{k,\delta}(H_{\rho_{0}}(z_{0}))}\leq c\rho_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}-(k+\delta)},\quad[\psi_{0}]_{C^{k,\delta}(H_{\rho_{0}}(z_{0}))}\leq c\rho_{0}^{2-(k+\delta)},\quad[\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1}]_{C^{k,\delta}(H_{\rho_{0}}(z_{0}))}\leq c\rho_{0}^{\frac{5}{2}-(k+\delta)}

with
\rho_{0}\coloneqq\frac{\max\{(x_{0,n})^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{0,n}|\}}{4}
, provided that
x_{0,n}\geq(v_{0,n})^{3}
.

From now on, for the convenience of notation, we always write

(2.9)

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(z)\coloneq\phi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n}),\ \varphi_{1}(z)\coloneq v_{n}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n}),\,\varphi_{2}(z)\coloneq\psi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n}),\,\varphi_{3}(z)\coloneq\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n}).

Lastly, we recall the vector space
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,\epsilon}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,\epsilon}\coloneqq\left\{p_{0}{\varphi_{0}}+p_{1}{\varphi_{1}}+p_{2}{\varphi_{2}}+p_{3}{\varphi_{3}}\,\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}p_{0},p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{[\lambda+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}]}\text{ with }p_{1}(0)=0,\\
p_{2}\in P_{[\lambda+\epsilon-2]},\,p_{3}\in P_{[\lambda+\epsilon-\frac{5}{2}]}\end{array}\right\}.

We are now ready to give the lemma for the expansion estimates at
\gamma_{0}
in one-sided cylinders.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
with
\lambda+\epsilon<3
, and
\lambda+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}\notin\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Let
A\in C^{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(H_{r})
,
B\in C^{\lambda-\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}(H_{r})
and
F\in C^{\lambda-2+\epsilon}(H_{r})
with
r\leq 1
and let
f
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}{f})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{r}$},\\
{f}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\big(\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}\big)\cap Q_{r}$}\end{aligned}\right.

with
(A(0))_{n,n}=1
. Then there exists
P\in\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,\epsilon}
such that for any
\rho\leq\frac{r}{2}
,

\displaystyle\|f-P\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\lambda+\epsilon}(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})}+r^{\lambda+\epsilon}[F]_{C^{\lambda-2+\epsilon}(H_{r})})

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(H_{r})},\|B\|_{C^{\lambda-\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}(H_{r})})
.

Proof.

Note that in
[
38
, Proposition 5.1]
, we have proved the same estimate when the domain is given by a two-sided kinetic cylinder
\mathcal{H}_{r}=(-r^{2},r^{2})\times(B_{r^{3}}\cap\{x_{n}>0\})\times B_{r}
. Every argument used in
[
38
, Section 5]
also works for the one-sided kinetic cylinder
H_{r}
, and the only difference is that the domain of the limiting equation in
[
38
, (5.38)]
is replaced by
(-\infty,0]\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover, all the Liouville theorems given in
[
38
, Section 4]
hold for the domain
(-\infty,0]\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, as we only used global
C^{\alpha}
estimates for kinetic equations and Liouville’s theorem for stationary solutions given in
[
38
, Section 3]
. Therefore, without changing any lines of the proof of
[
38
, Proposition 5.1]
, we can derive expansion estimates when the domain is given by the one-sided cylinder
H_{r}
.
∎

We end this section by providing pointwise estimates of the gradient in
v
of the solution up to the grazing set. This result is essentially a corollary of
subsection 2.2
and the regularity estimates of solutions up to
\gamma_{\pm}
(see
[
54
,
60
]
and
[
53
,
38
]
).

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
r>0
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
A\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})
and
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{r})
. Let
f
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}{f})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{r}$},\\
{f}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\big(\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}\big)\cap H_{r}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then for any
z_{1}\in H_{r/4}
,

(2.10)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}f(z_{1})|\leq c\max\{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{1,n}|\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}r^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})})
.

Proof.

We fix a point
z_{1}\in H_{r/4}
and divide the proof into three cases.

•

Assume
64x_{1,n}\leq(v_{1,n})^{3}
. Then we have

\displaystyle Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\subset\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad Q_{r}(z_{1})\subset H_{\overline{r}_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}),\quad\text{and}\quad H_{\overline{r}_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1})\subset H_{\rho_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1})

where
r_{1}\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{x_{1,n}}{v_{1,n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
\overline{r}_{1}\coloneqq 2(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}}
,
\overline{z}_{1}\coloneqq(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},0,v_{1})
and
\rho_{1}\coloneqq\frac{v_{1,n}}{2}
. Note that the point
\overline{z}_{1}
is a natural projection of the point
z_{1}
onto
\gamma_{-}
.
By a combination of
subsection 2.2
and
subsection 2.1
, we deduce

(2.11)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}f(z_{1})|\leq\|\nabla_{v}f\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r_{1}/2}(z_{1}))}\leq cr_{1}^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1}))}\leq cr_{1}^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\overline{r}_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}))}

for some
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r}(z_{0}))})
. Next, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(z)\coloneqq f(S_{\rho_{1}}z),\quad\widetilde{A}(z)\coloneqq A(S_{\rho_{1}}z),\quad\widetilde{B}(z)\coloneqq\rho_{1}B(S_{\rho_{1}}z)

to see that

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\widetilde{f}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(\widetilde{A}\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f})&=\widetilde{B}\cdot\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f}&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{1}(\widetilde{z}_{1})$},\\
\widetilde{f}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\widetilde{z}_{1}\coloneqq(t_{1}/\rho_{1}^{2},x_{1}^{\prime}/\rho_{1}^{3},0,v_{1}/\rho_{1})
. Since

\{x_{n}>0\}
is a convex domain and
v_{1}/\rho_{1}\cdot n_{x}=v_{1,n}/\rho_{1}=2
, by
[
54
, Theorem 1.3]
with
q=\frac{3}{2}
, we derive

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\overline{r}_{1}/\rho_{1}}(\widetilde{z}_{1}))}\leq c(\overline{r}_{1}/\rho_{1})^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}(\widetilde{z}_{1}))}=c(\overline{r}_{1}/\rho_{1})^{\frac{3}{2}}\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
. Furthermore, by
subsection 2.2
, there is a constant
c_{0}
such that for any
\rho\leq r/4
,

\displaystyle\|f-c_{0}{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}(z_{0}))}\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})})
, where we write
z_{0}\coloneqq(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},0,v_{1}^{\prime},0)
that is a natural projection of the point
\overline{z}_{1}
onto
\gamma_{0}
. In particular, we deduce that
|c_{0}|\leq r^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))}}
. Using this and the fact that
H_{\rho_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1})\subset H_{4\rho_{1}}(z_{0})
, we deduce

(2.13)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}))}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{4\rho_{1}}({z}_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq\|f-c_{0}{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{3\rho_{1}}({z}_{0}))}+c_{0}\|{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{4\rho_{1}}({z}_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq c({\rho_{1}}/{r})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})})
and we have used the fact that
\|{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{4\rho_{1}}({z}_{0}))}\leq c\rho_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}
by (
2.8
).
A combination of (
2.11
), (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) leads to

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}f(z_{1})|\leq c(rv_{1,n})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})},

which verifies (
2.10
).

•

Assume
64x_{1,n}\geq|v_{1,n}|^{3}
. Then we have

\displaystyle Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\subset\{x_{n}>0\},\quad Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\subset H_{32r_{1}}(z_{0})\subset H_{r/2}({z}_{0}),

where
r_{1}\coloneqq\frac{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}}}{16}
and
{z}_{0}\coloneqq(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},0,v_{1}^{\prime},0)
. Therefore, using interior estimates as in (
2.11
) and boundary estimates at
\gamma_{0}
as in (
2.13
), we deduce

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}f(z_{1})|\leq cr_{1}^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1}))}\leq cr_{1}^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{32r_{1}}(z_{0}))}\leq c(r_{1}r)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))},

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})})
, which implies (
2.10
).

•

Assume
64x_{1,n}\leq(-v_{1,n})^{3}
. Then we have

\displaystyle Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\subset\{x_{n}>0\},\quad Q_{r}(z_{1})\subset H_{\overline{r}_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}),\quad H_{\overline{r}_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1})\subset H_{\rho_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1})\subset H_{4\rho_{1}}(z_{0})\subset H_{r/2}(z_{0})

where
r_{1}\coloneqq\frac{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}}}{16}
,
\overline{r}_{1}\coloneqq 2(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}}
,
\overline{z}_{1}\coloneqq(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},0,v_{1})
,
\rho_{1}\coloneqq\frac{v_{1,n}}{2}
and
z_{0}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},0,v_{1}^{\prime},0)
. Similarly, by combining interior estimates as in (
2.11
), boundary estimates at
\gamma_{+}
as in (
2.12
) together with
subsection 2.2
and boundary estimates at
\gamma_{0}
as in (
2.13
), we have

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}f(z_{1})|\leq cr_{1}^{-1}\|f-f(\overline{z}_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1}))}\leq c\rho_{1}^{-1}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}))}\leq c\rho_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}r^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})})
. Indeed, we have applied interior estimates to the function
f(z)-f(\overline{z}_{1})
and we have used the fact that
\|f-f(\overline{z}_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r_{1}}(z_{1}))}\leq cr_{1}[f]_{C^{0,1}(H_{\overline{r}_{1}}(\overline{z}_{1}))}
.

Since we have proved (
2.10
) for each case, this completes the proof.
∎

3.
Propagation of positivity in kinetic geometry

In this section, we prove the propagation of positivity for kinetic equations via Harnack chains. In particular, we will show that the positivity of the solution
f
in a kinetic cylinder at time
t=0
follows from its positivity at a suitable past time (see
section 3
). It turns out that this past time cannot be chosen arbitrarily. This stands in stark contrast with the classical heat equation, where positivity at some time level
t=t_{0}
implies positivity of the solution for all later times
t>t_{0}
for all points in space.

Such a property fails in the kinetic setting. For example, there is a solution
f
in
Q_{1}
such that
f(-1/2,1/2,0)>0
, but
f(-1/4,0,0)=0
(see
[
12
, Proposition 4.5]
). Moreover, due to the kinetic geometry, we note in particular that propagation in the
x
-direction, from a point
z
depends on its velocity variable
v
.
Therefore, proving
section 3
requires a careful investigation. First, we determine how positivity propagates through Harnack inequalities (see
section 3
), and then, in a second step, we inductively construct paths from a given point to the kinetic cylinder where we aim to establish the positivity of the solution.

First, we recall Harnack’s inequality which was established in
[
24
, Theorem 5]
. Here, we state it in an appropriately rescaled form.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
f\geq 0
be a weak solution to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F\quad\text{in }Q_{1}.

Then there are universal small constants
r_{0}
and
\epsilon_{0}
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
and
\rho>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{r_{0}}}f(z_{1}\circ S_{\rho}z)\leq c\left(\inf_{Q_{r_{0}}(\epsilon_{0},0,0)}f(z_{1}\circ S_{\rho}z)+\rho^{2}\|F(z_{1}\circ S_{\rho}z)\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{1}(\epsilon_{0},0,0))}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, whenever
Q_{\rho}(z_{1}\circ(\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))\subset Q_{1}
. The two constants
r_{0},\epsilon_{0}
can be determined explicitly as follows:
r_{0}=\frac{1}{80}
and
\epsilon_{0}=\frac{19}{8}\frac{1}{400}
.

We next introduce an auxiliary lemma, which will be used in the construction of a Harnack chain. Given a set
D
, we describe the propagation set associated with each point
z\in D
via Harnack’s inequality.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
r_{0}
and
\epsilon_{0}
be the universal constants determined in
section 3
. Let
\rho\in(0,1)
,
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\geq 0
,
a\leq b
,
0<d_{1}\leq d_{2}
and a set
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\times B^{\prime}_{r_{1}}\times[d_{1},d_{2}]\times B^{\prime}_{r_{2}}\times[-r_{3},r_{3}]
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\coloneqq[a,b]
be given. Suppose

\displaystyle(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},v_{n})\in\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{B}\times B^{\prime}_{r_{2}+\rho r_{0}}\times[-r_{3}-\rho r_{0},r_{3}+\rho r_{0}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\coloneqq[a+\rho^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2}),b+\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\coloneq B^{\prime}_{r_{1}-\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{2}+(\rho r_{0})^{3}}\times[d_{1}+\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{3}-(\rho r_{0})^{3},d_{2}-\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{3}+(\rho r_{0})^{3}],

where we further assume that
r_{1}-\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{2}+(\rho r_{0})^{3}>0
and
d_{1}<d_{2}-2\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{3}+2(\rho r_{0})^{3}
, if
r_{2}>0
and
r_{3}>0
, respectively.
Then there is a point

\displaystyle(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1}^{\prime},v_{1,n})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\times B^{\prime}_{r_{1}}\times[d_{1},d_{2}]\times B^{\prime}_{r_{2}}\times[-r_{3},r_{3}]

such that

\displaystyle(t,x,v)\in Q_{\rho r_{0}}((t_{1},x_{1},v_{1})\circ(\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0)).

Proof.

Let us choose
(t_{1},x^{\prime}_{1},x_{1,n},v^{\prime}_{1},v_{1,n})
by

\displaystyle t_{1}\coloneqq\begin{cases}a&\quad\text{if }t<a+\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},\\
t-\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0}&\quad\text{if }t\geq a+\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},\end{cases}

\displaystyle v_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }|v^{\prime}|<\rho r_{0},\\
v^{\prime}-\rho r_{0}(\frac{v^{\prime}}{\|v^{\prime}\|})&\quad\text{if }|v^{\prime}|\geq\rho r_{0},\end{cases}

\displaystyle v_{1,n}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }|v_{n}|<\rho r_{0},\\
v_{n}-\rho r_{0}(\frac{v_{n}}{\|v_{n}\|})&\quad\text{if }|v_{n}|\geq\rho r_{0},\end{cases}

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }|x^{\prime}-(t-t_{1})v_{1}|<(\rho r_{0})^{3},\\
x^{\prime}-(t-t_{1})v^{\prime}_{1}-(\rho r_{0})^{3}\frac{x^{\prime}-(t-t_{1})v^{\prime}_{1}}{|x^{\prime}-(t-t_{1})v^{\prime}_{1}|}&\quad\text{if }|x^{\prime}-(t-t_{1})v_{1}|\geq(\rho r_{0})^{3},\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle x_{1,n}\coloneqq\begin{cases}d_{1}&\quad\text{if }x_{n}\leq d_{1}+(t-t_{1})v_{1,n}+(\rho r_{0})^{3},\\
x_{n}-(t-t_{1})v_{1,n}-(\rho r_{0})^{3}\frac{x_{n}-(t-t_{1})v_{1,n}}{|x_{n}-(t-t_{1})v_{1,n}|}&\quad\text{if }x_{n}>d_{1}+(t-t_{1})v_{1,n}+(\rho r_{0})^{3}.\end{cases}

Then we have
z\in Q_{\rho r_{0}}(z_{1}\circ(\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))
and
z_{1}\in[a,b]\times B^{\prime}_{r_{1}}\times[d_{1},d_{2}]\times B^{\prime}_{r_{2}}\times[-r_{3},r_{3}]
, where we have used the fact that
|t-t_{1}||v^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{2}
and
|t-t_{1}||v_{1,n}|\leq\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2}r_{3}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Before stating the main lemma, given
\varsigma\in(0,1/2)
and
r>0
, we introduce a kinetic cylinder
H_{r,\varsigma}^{+}
that stays away from
\{x_{n}=0\}
by

(3.1)

\displaystyle H_{r,\varsigma}^{+}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},v_{n})\in H_{r}\,:\,(\varsigma r)^{3}\leq x_{n}\leq r^{3}\}.

In particular, we write for any
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle H_{r,\varsigma}^{+}(t_{0},0,0)\coloneqq\{(t_{0},0,0)\circ z\,:\,z\in H^{+}_{r,\varsigma}\}.

Note that such cylinders will also be used to prove the Hopf lemma given in the next section.

Now, we are ready to prove the main result in this section. More precisely, when
f
is positive at some point, then its positivity propagates after a certain time gap. First we prove propagation only in the
x
-variables, while fixing the
v
-variables at
v=0
, which allows us to ignore the kinetic geometry. We then investigate propagation in the
v
-direction, which necessarily affects the propagation set in the
x
-direction because of the kinetic transport. However,
section 3
shows that when the cylinder on which we will apply Harnack’s inequality has a sufficiently small radius
\rho
, the displacement induced by the
v
-direction also stays small (see
Step 2
and
Step 4
in the proof of
section 3
for more details).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
r\in(0,1]
. For any
\delta\in(0,1]
, there is a constant
M=M(1/\delta)\geq 1
such that for any weak solution
f\geq 0
to

(3.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F\quad\text{in }I_{2rM}\times\mathrm{H}_{r(1+\delta)}

with
f(t_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},x_{0,n},v_{0})=f(-(rM)^{2},0,r^{3},0)=1
and for any
\varsigma\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
, if

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|r^{2}F\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{2rM}\times\mathrm{H}_{r(1+\delta)})}\leq 1/\mathcal{M}

for some constant
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,1/\delta,\varsigma)\geq 1
, then for any
a\in[-\frac{r^{2}}{16},0]
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{r}{2},{\varsigma}}(a,0,0)}f\geq\frac{1}{c},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma,1/\delta)
.

Note that when we apply this lemma later in
subsection 4.2
and
Theorem 4.3
, we will choose
\delta=1
or
\delta=\frac{1}{c}
for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, which has a uniform lower bound.

Proof.

First, note that for any
r\in(0,1]
, the rescaled function
\widetilde{f}(z)\coloneqq f(S_{r}z)
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{f}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{f}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(\widetilde{A}\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f})=\widetilde{B}\cdot\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f}+\widetilde{F}\quad\text{in }I_{2M}\times H_{1+\delta},

where
\widetilde{A}(z)\coloneqq A(S_{r}z)
,
\widetilde{B}(z)\coloneqq rB(S_{r}z)
, and
\widetilde{F}(z)\coloneqq r^{2}F(S_{r}z)
. Therefore, it suffices to prove the result in case
r=1
.

Fix two constants
M\geq 1
and
\mathcal{M}\geq 1
, which will be determined at the end of the proof (see (
3.22
) and (
3.23
)). Note from
section 3
that there are universal small constants
r_{0}
and
\epsilon_{0}
such that for any
\rho>0
,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{Q_{\rho r_{0}}(z_{1})}f(z)\leq C_{0}\left(\inf_{Q_{\rho r_{0}}(z_{1}\circ(\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))}f(z)+\rho^{2}\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{\rho}(z_{1}\circ(\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0)))}\right)

for some constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,\Lambda)
, whenever
Q_{\rho}(z_{1}\circ(\rho^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))\subset I_{2M}\times\mathrm{H}_{1+\delta}
.

We may assume
\delta\leq r_{0}
, as
f
is also a solution to (
3.2
) in
I_{2M}\times\mathrm{H}_{1+r_{0}}
, when
\delta>r_{0}
. As we will see, this is property would be enough to conclude the proof.

To prove the result, we will apply (
3.5
) in sequences of cylinders that are shifted consecutively in
x^{\prime}
,
v^{\prime}
,
x_{n}
, and
v_{n}
. Now we split the proof into four steps.

Step 1.
First, we are going to propagate positivity in
x^{\prime}
by moving forward in time. We prove that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},1,0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda)
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{k}\coloneqq[-M^{2}+k\delta^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2}),-M^{2}+k\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}]\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}}.

The case
k=1
follows directly from (
3.5
) with
\rho=\delta
and
z_{1}=(-M^{2},0,1,0)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Due to the choice of
\rho
, we have
Q_{\rho}(-M^{2},0,1,0)\subset I_{2M}\times\mathrm{H}_{1+\delta}
. When (
3.6
) with
k=l
holds, we use (
3.5
) with
\rho=\delta
and
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},1,0)
, where
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
to see that (
3.6
) with
k=l+1
, as for any
(t,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{l+1}
, there is a point
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
by
section 3
with
a=-M^{2}+l\delta^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2})
,
b=-M^{2}+l\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}
,
\rho=\delta
,
r_{1}=l(\delta r_{0})^{3}

r_{2}=r_{3}=0
,
d_{1}=d_{2}=1
. Thus, we have inductively proved (
3.6
) for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that at the end of the proof (see (
3.21
)) we will choose the positive integer
k=k(1/\delta)
to be sufficiently large.

Step 2.
Next, using (
3.6
) and (
3.5
), we will show for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{k,m}^{\prime}
,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+m}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},1,v^{\prime},0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k,m}\coloneqq[-M^{2}+(k+m)\delta^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2}),-M^{2}+(k+m)\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}]\times B^{\prime}_{r_{0}\delta^{3}(kr_{0}^{2}-\epsilon_{0}\frac{(m-1)m}{2})}\times B_{m\delta r_{0}}^{\prime},

i.e. we spread positivity further in the
v^{\prime}
direction.
First, using (
3.5
) with
\rho=\delta
and
z_{1}=(t,x^{\prime},1,0)
, where
(t,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
, we have (
3.7
) for
m=1
. Then we assume (
3.7
) with
m=l
. Before applying (
3.5
), we note that for any
(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k,l+1}
, there is a point
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k,l}
such that

(3.8)

\displaystyle(t,x^{\prime},1,v^{\prime},0)\in Q_{\delta r_{0}}((t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},1,v_{1}^{\prime},0)\circ(\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0)),

by
section 3
with
\rho=\delta
,
a=-M^{2}+(k+l)\delta^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2})
,
b=-M^{2}+(k+l)\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}
,
r_{1}=r_{0}\delta^{3}(kr_{0}^{2}-\epsilon_{0}\frac{(l-1)l}{2})
,
d_{1}=d_{2}=1
,
r_{2}=l\delta r_{0}
.
Therefore, for any
(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k,l+1}
, we employ (
3.5
) with
\rho=\delta
and
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},1,v_{1}^{\prime},0)
, where the point
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{k,l}
is determined in (
3.8
) so that
(
3.7
) is true for
m=l+1
. This proves (
3.7
).

To end this step, we finally choose the integer
m=m(1/\delta)
large enough so that

(3.9)

1\leq m\delta r_{0}<2.

As
r_{0}=\frac{1}{80}
by
section 3
,
m
only depends on
1/\delta
.
This gives that
\epsilon_{0}m^{2}\delta^{3}r_{0}\leq\epsilon_{0}(m\delta r_{0})^{2}\leq 4\epsilon_{0}\leq 1
, as we assumed
\delta\leq r_{0}
. Thus, we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+m}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},1,v^{\prime},0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}},

where

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{k,m}\coloneqq[-M^{2}+(k+m)\delta^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2}),-M^{2}+(k+m)\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}]\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-1}\times B_{1}^{\prime}.

Step 3.

In this step we propagate positivity in the
x_{n}
-variable from
\{x_{n}=1\}
towards
\{x_{n}=0\}
. We will do so in two steps. First, we have to apply the Harnack inequality to kinetic cylinders of radius
\delta
, in order to move away from the boundary of the solution domain
\{x_{n}=(1+\delta)^{3}\}
. Once we have left the boundary scale, in as second step, we move further by taking cylinders of radius
|x_{n}|^{\frac{1}{3}}
. This choice guarantees that we stay away from the boundary
\{x_{n}=0\}
when
x_{n}
is close to
0
. We will divide the proof into two cases.

Step 3-1.
First, we prove the lower bound when
x_{n}
is close to
x_{n}=(1+\delta)^{3}
.
We will prove for any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}

(3.12)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+m+\mathbf{n}}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m+\mathbf{n}}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{I}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-1-\mathbf{n}\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}}\times[1-\mathbf{n}(\delta r_{0})^{3},1]\times B_{1}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq[-M^{2}+((k+m)\delta^{2}+\mathbf{n}\delta^{2})(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2}),-M^{2}+((k+m)\delta^{2}+\mathbf{n}\delta^{2})\epsilon_{0}].

First, we observe from
section 3
with
\rho=\delta
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}
,
r_{1}=k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-1-\mathbf{n}\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0}
,
d_{1}=1-\mathbf{n}(\delta r_{0})^{3}
,
d_{2}=1
,
r_{2}=1
, and
r_{3}=0
that for any
(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}
, there is a point
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}-1}
such that

\displaystyle(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},0)\in Q_{\delta r_{0}}((t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v^{\prime},0)\circ(\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0)).

In addition, we note
Q_{\delta}((t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v^{\prime},0)\circ(\delta^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))\subset I_{2M}\times\mathrm{H}_{1+\delta}
.
Based on this observation, the estimate (
3.12
) with
\mathbf{n}=1
is obtained by a combination of (
3.7
) and (
3.5
) with
\rho=\delta
and
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},1,v_{1}^{\prime},0)
for any
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{k,m}
. Now, we assume (
3.12
) with
\mathbf{n}=l
for some positive integer
l
. Then we do the same procedure with
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1}^{\prime},0)
for any
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}_{k,m,l}
, so we get (
3.12
) when
\mathbf{n}=l+1
. This proves (
3.12
).

To conclude this step, we let
\mathbf{n}_{1}
be the positive integer
\mathbf{n}_{1}=\mathbf{n}_{1}(1/\delta)
such that

(3.13)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}<\mathbf{n}_{1}(\delta r_{0})^{3}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Thus, we have proved

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{on }\mathcal{I}^{1}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}_{1}}\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-1-\mathbf{n}_{1}}\times\left[\frac{3}{4},\frac{7}{8}\right]\times B_{1}^{\prime}.

Step 3-2.
Next, we show that for any
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{A}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{I}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-1-\mathbf{n}_{1}-\epsilon_{0}\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}}}\times\left[(\rho_{\mathbf{n}})^{3},7/8\right]\times B_{1}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left[-M^{2}+(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2})\left((k+m+\mathbf{n}_{1})\delta^{2}+\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}}\right),-M^{2}+\epsilon_{0}\left((k+m+\mathbf{n}_{1})\delta^{2}+\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}}\right)\right]

with

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{n}}\coloneqq\left(\frac{3}{4}(1-r_{0}^{3})^{\mathbf{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{\mathbf{i}=0}^{\mathbf{n}-1}\rho_{\mathbf{i}}^{2}.

First, note that for any
(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}
, there is
(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}-1}
such that

\displaystyle(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},0)\in Q_{\rho_{\mathbf{n}-1}r_{0}}((t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1}^{\prime},0)\circ((\rho_{\mathbf{n}-1})^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))

and
Q_{\rho_{\mathbf{n}-1}}((t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1}^{\prime},0)\circ((\rho_{\mathbf{n}-1})^{2}\epsilon_{0},0,0))\subset I_{2M}\times\mathrm{H}_{1+\delta}
, which follows from
section 3
with
\rho=\rho_{\mathbf{n}-1}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}=\mathcal{I}^{0}_{k,m,\mathbf{n}}
,
r_{1}=k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-2-\mathbf{n}_{1}-\mathbf{n}
,
d_{1}=(\rho_{\mathbf{n}-1})^{3}
,
d_{2}=7/8
,
r_{2}=1
, and
r_{3}=0
. Therefore, as in (
3.12
),
we deduce (
3.14
) with
\mathbf{n}=l+1
from a combination of (
3.5
) with
\rho=\rho_{l}
and (
3.14
) with
\mathbf{n}=l
. This proves (
3.14
).

To conclude this step, we apply (
3.14
) with
\mathbf{n}
being the positive integer
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}(\varsigma)
such that

(3.15)

\displaystyle(\rho_{\mathbf{n}})^{3}=\frac{3}{4}(1-r_{0}^{3})^{\mathbf{n}}\leq\left(\frac{\varsigma}{4}\right)^{3}.

Therefore, we have proved

(3.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k}}\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq f(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},0)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{A},

with

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=[-M^{2}+(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2})(k\delta^{2}+R_{0}),-M^{2}+\epsilon_{0}(k\delta^{2}+R_{0})]\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-R_{1}}\times[(\varsigma/4)^{3},7/8]\times B_{1}^{\prime}

for some constants
R_{0},R_{1}
depending only on
1/\delta
and some constant
c_{0}=c_{0}(n,\Lambda,\varsigma,1/\delta)
, where we have used (
3.9
), (
3.13
) and the fact that
\delta\leq r_{0}
. To be more precise, we have chosen

\displaystyle R_{0}=(m+\mathbf{n}_{1})\delta^{2}+\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}},\quad R_{1}=1+\mathbf{n}_{1},\quad c_{0}=C_{0}^{m+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}}.

The integer
\mathbf{n}
depends on
\varsigma
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\mathbf{n}}=\sum_{\mathbf{i}=0}^{\mathbf{n}-1}(\rho_{\mathbf{i}})^{2}\leq\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{1}{1-(1-r_{0}^{3})^{\frac{2}{3}}},

which has a universal upper bound independent of
\mathbf{n}
.

Step 4.

We now apply the Harnack inequality once more in order to propagate positivity in the
v_{n}
-variable. Note that we choose cylinders of radius
\varrho\leq(\frac{\varsigma}{4})^{3}
, which allows us to control the influence of the velocity variables along the Harnack chain. As a consequence, the positivity of
f
will be preserved in the region
x_{n}\in[(\varsigma/2)^{3},3/4]
.

Now, using (
3.16
), we will prove for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+j}}\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq f(z)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}+j}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{I}_{j}\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-R_{1}-j(\varrho/4)^{2}\epsilon_{0}}\times\left[(\varsigma/4)^{3}+\widetilde{r}_{j},7/8-\widetilde{r}_{j}\right]\times B_{1}^{\prime}\times[-j(\varrho r_{0}/4),j(\varrho r_{0}/4)],

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq[-M^{2}+(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2})(k\delta^{2}+R_{0}+j(\varrho/4)^{2}),-M^{2}+\epsilon_{0}(k\delta^{2}+R_{0}+j(\varrho/4)^{2})],

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\epsilon_{0}\frac{j(j+1)}{2}(\varrho/4)^{3}r_{0}

with
\varrho\coloneqq\min\{r_{0}(\varsigma/4)^{3},1/2^{10}\}
.
To do so, we observe that for any
z\in\mathcal{B}_{j}
, there is a point
z_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{j-1}
such that

\displaystyle z\in{Q}_{\varrho r_{0}/4}(z_{1}\circ((\varrho/4)^{2}\epsilon,0,0))\quad\text{and}\quad{Q}_{\varrho/4}(z_{1}\circ((\varrho/4)^{2}\epsilon,0,0))\subset I_{2M}\times\mathrm{H}_{1+\delta},

by
section 3
with
\rho=\varrho/4
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}=\mathcal{I}_{j-1}
,
r_{1}=k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-R_{1}-(j-1)(\varrho/4)^{2}(\epsilon_{0}-r_{0}^{2})
,
d_{1}=(\varsigma/4)^{3}+\widetilde{r}_{j-1}

d_{2}=7/8-\widetilde{r}_{j-1}
,
r_{2}=1
and
r_{3}=(j-1)(\varrho r_{0}/4)
.
Therefore, by following the same procedure as in
Step 3
, we deduce (
3.18
).

Now we let
j
be the positive integer
j=j(\varsigma)
such that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\leq j(\varrho r_{0}/4)<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{10}}.

Thus, this together with the fact that
\varrho\leq r_{0}(\varsigma/4)^{3}
implies

(3.20)

\displaystyle\frac{j(j+1)}{2}(\varrho/4)^{3}r_{0}\leq{j^{2}}(\varrho r_{0}/4)^{2}(\varsigma/4)^{3}\leq(\varsigma/4)^{3}\quad\text{and}\quad j(\varrho/4)^{2}\leq j\varrho r_{0}/4\leq 1.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{0}^{k+j}}\frac{1}{c_{0}}\leq f(z)+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1+m+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{I}_{j}\times B^{\prime}_{k(\delta r_{0})^{3}-R_{1}-1}\times[(\varsigma/2)^{3},3/4]\times B_{1/2}

for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,\Lambda,\varsigma,k,\delta)
.

Step 5.
Now, we are in a position to conclude the proof. We let
k
be the positive integer
k=k(1/\delta)
such that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\frac{9}{8}+R_{1}\leq k(\delta r_{0})^{3}<2+R_{1},

which gives

\displaystyle k(\delta r_{0})^{2}\geq 1,

where the constant
R_{1}=R_{1}(1/\delta)
is given in (
3.17
). We are now going to select
M
and
\mathcal{M}
. First, choosing
M
by

(3.22)

M\coloneqq\sqrt{\epsilon_{0}(k\delta^{2}+R_{0}+j(\varrho/4)^{2})}^{\frac{1}{2}},

which depends only on
1/\delta
by (
3.21
), (
3.17
), and (
3.20
), we deduce
(-1,0]\subset\mathcal{I}_{j}
,
as
k(\delta r_{0})^{2}\geq 1
. Next, we select
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,1/\delta,\varsigma)
such that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{C_{0}^{i}\mathcal{M}}=\frac{C_{0}}{C_{0}-1}\frac{1}{\mathcal{M}}\leq\frac{1}{2c_{1}},

where
c_{1}\coloneqq C_{0}^{k+j}c_{0}
, which implies

(3.24)

\displaystyle\inf_{a\in[0,\frac{1}{16}]}\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{1}{2},\varsigma}(-a,0,0)}f\geq\frac{1}{c}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,1/\delta,\varsigma)
. Thus, we have proved (
3.4
), which completes the proof.
∎

We conclude this section by presenting an auxiliary lemma that will be used in the next section. It shows that positivity propagates even up to the boundary of the solution domain at outgoing boundary points
\gamma_{+}
. The main idea of the proof is to suitably extend the solution across
\gamma_{+}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
r\in(0,1]
and let
f\geq 0
be a weak solution to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F\quad\text{in }H_{4r}.

Let
\varsigma\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and assume

\displaystyle\inf_{H^{+}_{2r,\varsigma}(-a,0,0)}f\geq c_{0}

for some
c_{0}>0
and
a\in[0,r^{2}]
. Then there is a constant
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,\frac{a}{\varsigma^{2}})\geq 1
such that if

\displaystyle\|r^{2}F\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{4r})}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{\mathcal{M}},

then

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{A}_{r,2\varsigma},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma)
and we write

(3.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{r,2\varsigma}\coloneqq{H}_{r}\cap\left[\Big(\{0\leq x_{n}\leq(2r\varsigma)^{3}\}\times\{-r\leq v_{n}\leq-2r\varsigma\}\Big)\cup\Big(\{x_{n}=(2r\varsigma)^{3}\}\times\{-r\leq v_{n}\leq 0\}\Big)\right].

Proof.

By scaling, we may assume
r=1
. First, fix a constant
\mathcal{M}\geq 1
, which will be determined at the end of the proof. Next note from
[
54
]
that
f
is Hölder continuous up to the boundary, so that we can assume that
f\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{Q})
, where

\displaystyle Q\coloneqq\{(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},v_{n})\,:(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in Q^{\prime}_{3},\,v_{n}\in[-3-\varsigma,-\varsigma],\,x_{n}\in[-3,3]\}

and
f\geq 0
.
Thus, as in the proof of
[
59
, Cor 2.9]
together with
\nabla_{v}f\in L^{2}(Q)
and
f\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{Q})
, we have a unique weak solution
f_{0}\geq 0
to

(3.26)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f_{0}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{0}-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}_{0}\nabla_{v}{f_{0}})&=B_{0}\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{0}+F_{0}&&\qquad\mbox{in $Q$},\\
{f_{0}}&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}Q$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
A_{0}=A
,
B_{0}=B
,
F_{0}=F
on
H_{4}
and
A_{0}\equiv I
,
B_{0}\equiv 0
,
F_{0}\equiv 0
on
(H_{4})^{c}
.
On the other hand, since
\{x_{n}=0\}\cap\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}(Q\cap\{x_{n}>0\})=\emptyset
, we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}(f-f_{0})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}(f-f_{0})-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}(f-{f_{0}}))&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}(f-f_{0})&&\qquad\mbox{in $Q\cap\{x_{n}>0\}$},\\
f-f_{0}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}(Q\cap\{x_{n}>0\})$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Hence by the uniqueness of solutions to this problem, we get
f=f_{0}
in
Q\cap\{x_{n}\geq 0\}
.

Thus, we deduce that
f
is also a solution to (
3.26
), and we will apply Harnack’s inequality iteratively to the equation (
3.26
). This yields for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.27)

\displaystyle f_{0}(z_{1})

\displaystyle\leq c\big(f_{0}(t_{1}+\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},x_{1}+v_{1}\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},v_{1})+c_{0}/\mathcal{M}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\cdots\leq c^{k}f_{0}(t_{1}+k\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},x_{1}+kv_{1}\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},v_{1})+c_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{k}c^{i}/\mathcal{M}

for some constants
\epsilon_{0}
and
c=c(n,\Lambda)
,
whenever
Q_{\rho}(t_{1}+k\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},x_{1}+kv_{1}\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},v_{1})\subset Q\cup H_{4}
. Next, we choose
\rho\coloneqq\frac{\varsigma}{4}
so that
Q_{{\rho}}(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\subset Q\cup H_{4}
for every
(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2\varsigma}
. Moreover, there is a positive integer
k=k(\frac{a}{\varsigma^{2}})
such that
\frac{64}{\epsilon_{0}}+\frac{a}{\epsilon_{0}}(\frac{4}{\varsigma})^{2}\leq k\leq\frac{128}{\epsilon_{0}}+\frac{a}{\epsilon_{0}}(\frac{4}{\varsigma})^{2}
. Thus, for any
(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2\varsigma}
, we have

\displaystyle(t_{2}-k\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},x_{2}-kv_{2}\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},v_{2})\in H_{2,\varsigma}^{+}(-a,0,0).

In particular, when
a=0
, the positive integer
k
is a universal constant.
Thus, by (
3.27
) for any
(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2\varsigma}
, we get

(3.28)

\displaystyle c_{0}\leq f_{0}(t_{2}-k\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},x_{2}-kv_{2}\epsilon_{0}\rho^{2},v_{2})\leq c\big(f_{0}(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})+c_{0}/\mathcal{M}\big)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\frac{a}{\varsigma^{2}})
. Now by choosing
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,\frac{a}{\varsigma^{2}})
large enough, we get the desired result from the fact that
f_{0}(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})=f(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})
since
(t_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2\varsigma}\subset Q\cap\{x_{n}>0\}
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.
Hopf lemma

In this section, we prove a Hopf lemma for a general class of kinetic equations. We proceed in several steps. First, we show the result for solutions to Kolmogorov’s equation (with constant coefficients) and then, in a second step, we develop a perturbation argument which allows us to prove the result for kinetic Fokker-Planck equations with Hölder continuous coefficients and bounded source terms (see
Theorem 4.3
).

Recall the 1D function
{\varphi_{0}}
, which is introduced in (
2.9
) and solves (
1.5
). In addition, from
[
38
, Lemma C.1 and Lemma C.5]
, we have

(4.1)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varphi_{0}}\geq 0,\quad\partial_{v}{\varphi_{0}}\leq 0,\quad{\varphi_{0}}(x,v)\leq c\max\{|x|^{\frac{1}{3}},|v|\}^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varphi_{0}}(x,v)\geq\frac{1}{c}\max\{|x|^{\frac{1}{3}},|v|\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{whenever }v\leq x^{3}.

4.1.
Hopf lemma for Kolmogorov’s equation with constant coefficients

First, we define a time-dependent kinetic cylinder
\mathcal{H}_{a,b}
with
a\geq b
, which is has radius
a
in
t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime}
and
b
in
x_{n},v_{n}
, and is given by

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{a,b}\coloneqq\{z\in H_{a}\,:\,x_{n}\in[0,b^{3}],\,v_{n}\in[-b,b]\}=(-a^{2},0]\times(B_{a^{3}}^{\prime}\times[0,b^{3}])\times(B_{a}^{\prime}\times[-b,b]),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a,b}\coloneqq\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{a,b}\cap\Big(\left[\{x_{n}=b^{3}\}\times\{-b^{3}\leq v_{n}\leq 0\}\right]\cup\left[\{0\leq x_{n}\leq b^{3}\}\times\{v_{n}=-b\}\right]\Big).

In
section 3
, we have proved that for positivity to be propagated up to time level
t=t_{0}
, we need to assume positivity at time
t=t_{0}-M^{2}
for some constant
M
, which cannot be arbitrarily small. This motivates to introduce the aforementioned cylinders
\mathcal{H}_{a,b}
.

With this cylinder, we construct a barrier that serves as a subsolution.

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists a large constant
M=M(n,\Lambda)\geq 8
such that there is a solution
\psi_{1}
to

(4.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\psi_{1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi_{1}-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}\psi_{1}&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{M,1}$},\\
\psi_{1}&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{on $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{M,1}\setminus\Gamma_{M,1}$},\\
\psi_{1}&\leq c&&\qquad\mbox{on $\Gamma_{M,1}$},\\
\psi_{1}(t,x,v)&\geq{\varphi_{0}}(x_{n},v_{n})/2&&\qquad\mbox{in $(t,x,v)\in{H}_{1/2}$}\end{aligned}\right.

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, where
a^{n,n}=1
.

Proof.

We fix
M\geq 1
, which will be determined later in (
4.6
). Next, we consider cut-off functions
\xi_{1}=\xi_{1}(t)
and
\xi_{2}=\xi_{2}(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})
such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\xi_{1}(t)=0\text{ for }t\in[-1,0],\quad\xi_{1}(-M^{2})=-8,\quad\xi_{1}(t)\in[-8,0],\quad|\partial_{t}\xi_{1}|\leq\frac{c}{M^{2}}

and

(4.5)

\displaystyle\xi_{2}\equiv 0\text{ on }\mathrm{Q}_{1}^{\prime},\quad\xi_{2}\equiv-8\text{ on }\partial\mathrm{Q}^{\prime}_{M},\quad\xi_{2}\in[-8,0],\quad|D^{k}\xi_{2}|\leq\frac{c}{M^{k}}

for some constant
c=c(n)
, where
k\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\mathrm{Q}^{\prime}_{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
is a stationary cylinder defined in (
2.1
). Then we set

\displaystyle\psi_{1}\coloneqq(\xi_{1}(t)+\xi_{2}(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})){\varphi_{0}}+{\varphi_{0}}(v_{n}-1)^{2}+(1-v_{n}){\varphi_{0}},

where
{\varphi_{0}}\equiv{\varphi_{0}}(x_{n},v_{n})
, to see that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{1}=(\partial_{t}\xi_{1}){\varphi_{0}},

\displaystyle v_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\psi_{1}=v_{i}(\partial_{x_{i}}\xi_{2}){\varphi_{0}},\quad-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}\psi_{1}=-a^{i,j}(\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}\xi_{2}){\varphi_{0}}\quad\text{for any }i,j\neq n,

\displaystyle v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}\psi_{1}-a^{n,n}\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}\psi_{1}=-2{\varphi_{0}}-4\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}(v_{n}-1)+2\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},

\displaystyle-a^{i,n}\partial_{v_{i},v_{n}}\psi_{1}=-a^{i,n}(\partial_{v_{i}}\xi_{2})(\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}),

where we have used (
1.5
). Now using this, together with (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we derive

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi_{1}-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},vj}\psi_{1}\leq-2{\varphi_{0}}+2\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}+c\left(\frac{{\varphi_{0}}}{M^{2}}+\frac{|\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}|}{M}\right)\quad\text{ in }\mathcal{H}_{M,1}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
. Since
{\varphi_{0}}\geq 0
,
\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}\leq 0
, and using also that
a^{n,n}=1
, there is a constant
M=M(n,\Lambda)\geq 8
such that

(4.6)

\displaystyle-2{\varphi_{0}}+2\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}+c\left(\frac{{\varphi_{0}}}{M^{2}}+\frac{|\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}|}{M}\right)\leq-{\varphi_{0}}+\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}\leq 0,

which gives that
\psi_{1}
satisfies the first condition given in (
4.3
).

Now we will check the remaining three conditions in (
4.3
). We observe

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(-M^{2},x,v)\leq-8{\varphi_{0}}+{\varphi_{0}}(v_{n}-1)^{2}+(1-v_{n}){\varphi_{0}}\leq-2{\varphi_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq 0,\quad\text{for any }(-M^{2},x,v)\in\mathcal{H}_{M,1},

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t,x,v)\leq-8{\varphi_{0}}+{\varphi_{0}}(v_{n}-1)^{2}+(1-v_{n}){\varphi_{0}}\leq-2{\varphi_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq 0\quad\text{in }\{z\in\overline{\mathcal{H}_{M,1}}\,:\,(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\partial\mathrm{Q}^{\prime}_{M}\},
