\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t,x^{\prime},0,v^{\prime},v_{n})

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{in }\{(t,x^{\prime},0,v^{\prime},v_{n})\in\mathcal{H}_{M,1}\,:\,v_{n}>0\}

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},1)

\displaystyle\leq 0\quad\text{in }\{(t,x^{\prime},x_{n},v^{\prime},1)\in\mathcal{H}_{M,1}\},

\displaystyle|\psi_{1}(z)|\leq 24|{\varphi_{0}}(x_{n},v_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq c\quad\text{ in }\mathcal{H}_{M,1},

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(t,x,v)\geq{\varphi_{0}}(v_{n}-1)^{2}+(1-v_{n}){\varphi_{0}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{{\varphi_{0}}}{2}\quad\text{in }{H}_{\frac{1}{2}}

where we have used
\xi_{1}{\varphi_{0}},\xi_{2}{\varphi_{0}}\leq 0
. This completes the proof.
∎

With
subsection 4.1
at hand, we are now ready to prove the main result in this subsection. This result was already established for stationary equations in 1D in
[
38
, Lemma 3.7]
. Here, we are using the new barrier function from
subsection 4.1
and the propagation of positivity result from
section 3
to extend it to the non-stationary equation in higher dimension.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
h\geq 0
be a weak solution to

(4.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}{h}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{4r}$},\\
h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$}\end{aligned}\right.

with
a^{n,n}=1
. There are constants
c=c(n,\Lambda)
and
\sigma=\sigma(n,\Lambda)\leq\frac{1}{4}
such that

\displaystyle\inf_{H_{2r,\sigma}^{+}(-r^{2},0,0)}\frac{h}{{\varphi_{0}}}\leq c\inf_{H_{\sigma r}}\frac{h}{{\varphi_{0}}}.

Proof.

We may assume
r=1
. First, fix the positive integer
M=M(n,\Lambda)\geq 8
and the function
\psi_{1}
, which are determined in
subsection 4.1
. Then we define
\varphi(z)\coloneqq\psi_{1}(S_{1/M}z)
to see that

(4.8)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\varphi+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}{\varphi}&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{1,1/M}$},\\
{\varphi}&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{on $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{1,1/M}\setminus\Gamma_{1,1/M}$},\\
\varphi&\leq c&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Gamma_{1,1/M}$},\\
\varphi&\geq\frac{{\varphi_{0}}}{c}&&\qquad\text{in }{H}_{1/(2M)}\end{aligned}\right.

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, as
M
depends only on
n,\Lambda
, where the sets
\mathcal{H}_{1,1/M}
and
\Gamma_{1,1/M}
are defined in (
4.2
).

Now we fix
\sigma\coloneqq\frac{1}{2M}
. We will prove the lower bound of the function
h
on
\Gamma_{1,2\sigma}
. Next, we may set
m_{0}\coloneqq\inf\limits_{H_{2,\sigma}^{+}(-1,0,0)}\frac{h}{{\varphi_{0}}}\neq 0
. This gives

(4.9)

\displaystyle h\geq\frac{m_{0}}{c_{0}}\quad\text{in }H_{2,\sigma}^{+}(-1,0,0)

for some constant
c_{0}=c_{0}(n,\Lambda)
, as
{\varphi_{0}}(x,v)\geq\frac{1}{c_{0}}
when
x\geq(2\sigma)^{3}
by (
4.1
). By applying
section 3
with
r=1
,
\varsigma=\sigma(=\frac{1}{2M})
, and
a=1
, we have

(4.10)

\displaystyle h\geq\frac{m_{0}}{c}\quad\text{in }\Gamma_{1,2\sigma}\subset\mathcal{A}_{1,2\sigma}.

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, as the constant
\sigma
depends only on
n,\Lambda
.

Now, we are ready to apply the maximum principle to get the desired lower bound of
h
in
{H}_{\sigma}\cap\{t=0\}
.
By (
4.8
) and (
4.10
), we have that there is a constant
c_{3}=c_{3}(n,\Lambda)
such that
\widetilde{h}\coloneqq\frac{m_{0}\varphi}{c_{3}}-h
is a solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\widetilde{h}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{h}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}\widetilde{h})&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{1,1/M}$},\\
\widetilde{h}&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{1,1/M}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Thus, by the maximum principle, we have
\frac{m_{0}}{c_{3}}\varphi\leq h
in
\mathcal{H}_{1,1/M}
. In particular, we deduce

(4.11)

\displaystyle\left(\inf_{H^{+}_{2,\sigma}(-1,0,0)}\frac{h}{{\varphi_{0}}}\right){\varphi_{0}}(x_{n},v_{n})\leq ch(t,x,v)\quad\text{for any }(t,x,v)\in H_{\sigma}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, where we have used that
\frac{m_{0}\varphi}{c_{3}}\geq\frac{m_{0}}{c}{\varphi_{0}}
in
H_{\sigma}
for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, which follows from the last condition of (
4.8
) and
\sigma=\frac{1}{2M}
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.2.
Hopf lemma for a general class of kinetic Fokker-Planck equations

In this subsection, we consider more general kinetic equations and prove a Hopf lemma of such equations via a perturbation argument.

The main result of this subsection reads as follows.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
f\geq 0
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{2}$},\\
f&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{2}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
A\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})
,
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{2})
,
0\leq F\in L^{\infty}(H_{2})
for some
\epsilon>0
. We assume
(A(0))_{n,n}=1
and

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}\leq 1.

Then there exist a universal constant
\rho
and a large constant
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda)
such that if
f(z_{0})=c_{0}>0
for some
c_{0}>0
with
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},x_{0,n},v_{0})=(-1,0,\rho^{3},0)
and

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{\mathcal{M}},

then

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{c}\leq\inf_{H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}}\frac{f}{{\varphi_{0}}},

where
c=c(n,\epsilon,\Lambda,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})})
.

We recall the notation
\mathcal{R}_{-}\coloneqq\{x_{n}\leq v_{n}^{3}\}
. Note that we do not establish the comparability of
f
and
{\varphi_{0}}
inside
\mathcal{R}_{-}
. This is due to the fact that
{\varphi_{0}}
exhibits exponential decay at the boundary inside this region, which is in stark contrast to the polynomial growth away from that set (see (
1.5
)). Due to the exponential decay of
{\varphi_{0}}
, the perturbation argument that our proof is based on no longer works. We do not know, whether the result of
Theorem 4.3
holds in
\mathcal{R}_{-}
, when
A
is non-constant and
B,F\not=0
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
f\geq 0
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{2}$},\\
f&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{2}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
A\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})
and
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{2})
with
(A(0))_{n,n}=1
. We assume

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}\leq 1.

Then there is a universal constant
\rho\leq 1
such that if
f(z_{0})=c_{0}
for some constant
c_{0}>0
with
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},x_{0,n},v_{0})=(-1,0,\rho^{3},0)
, then

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{c}\leq\inf_{H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}}\frac{f}{{\varphi_{0}}},

where
c=c(n,\epsilon,\Lambda,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})})
.

The main idea of the proof is to compare
f
with a solution
h
to Kolmogorov’s equation with constant coefficients and to apply
subsection 4.1
.

Proof.

Let
M
be the constant from
section 3
applied with
\delta=1
and fix
\rho=\frac{1}{M}
. Then by
section 3
applied with
r=\rho
, we have for any

\varsigma\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
,

\displaystyle\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma}}{f}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma)
. In addition, by
section 3
applied with
F\equiv 0
,
a=0
, and
r=\frac{\rho}{4}
,

(4.12)

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}\quad\text{on }H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma)
.

Next, we consider the weak solution
h_{1}
to

(4.13)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h_{1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h_{1}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}h_{1})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{1}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{r,\frac{r}{N}}$},\\
h_{1}&=1&&\qquad\mbox{on $\Gamma_{r,\frac{r}{N}}$},\\
h_{1}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{on $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{r,\frac{r}{N}}\setminus\Gamma_{r,\frac{r}{N}}$}\end{aligned}\right.

for some small constant
r\in(0,1)
and large constant
N\geq 1
, which will be determined later (see (
4.30
) and (
4.20
), respectively). Note that the sets
\mathcal{H}_{r,\frac{r}{N}}
and
\Gamma_{r,\frac{r}{N}}
are defined in (
4.2
). In particular, we will always choose
N
,
r\leq\frac{\rho}{4}
, and then
\varsigma=\frac{2r}{N\rho}
, so that
\Gamma_{r,\frac{r}{N}}\subset H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},{2\varsigma}}
. Due to (
4.12
) and the construction of
h_{1}
, we can apply the maximum principle, which yields

(4.14)

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}h_{1}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{H}_{r,\frac{r}{N}}.

The idea of this construction is to reduce the proof of the Hopf lemma for
f
to the proof of a Hopf lemma for
h_{1}
, for which the values at
\Gamma_{r,\frac{r}{N}}
are normalized to one.

Step 1.
We will derive a positive lower bound for
h_{1}
away from the boundary
\{x_{n}=0\}
for each time level
t\in[-(r/4)^{2},0]
, which is needed for
Step 2
(see (
4.20
) below). To do so, we observe from the maximum principle that
h_{1}\leq 1
and by
[
54
]
, for any
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1})=(t_{1},0,(\frac{r}{N})^{3},0)
with
t_{1}\in[-(\frac{r}{2})^{2},0]
,

(r/N)^{\beta}[h_{1}]_{C^{\beta}(H_{{r/(2N)}}(z_{1}))}\leq c,

where
c=c(n,\Lambda)
and
\beta=\beta(n,\Lambda)\in(0,1)
. By choosing a sufficiently small constant
a_{0}=a_{0}(n,\Lambda)\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, we derive for any
t_{1}\in[-(\frac{r}{2})^{2},0]
,

(4.15)

\displaystyle{h}_{1}(t_{1},0,(r/N)^{3}(1-a_{0}),0)\geq{h}_{1}(z_{1})-|{h}(z_{1})-{h}_{1}(t_{1},0,(r/N)^{3}(1-a_{0}),0)|\geq 1-ca_{0}^{\frac{\beta}{3}}\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Next, note that
h_{1}
solves

(4.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{h}_{1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}{h}_{1}-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}{h}_{1})={B}\cdot\nabla_{v}{h}_{1}\quad\text{in }I_{\frac{r}{2}}\times\mathrm{H}_{\frac{r}{N}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{1}{3}}(1+\delta_{0})}.

Now let
M_{0}=M_{0}(1/\delta_{0})
be the large constant determined in
section 3
with
F\equiv 0
,
\delta=\delta_{0}
and
\varsigma=\frac{1}{2}
. By assuming
N\geq 4M_{0}
and (
4.15
), we have for
\widetilde{t}_{1}=-(\frac{rM_{0}}{N})^{2}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle{h}_{1}(-\widetilde{t}_{1},0,(r/N)^{3}(1-a_{0}),0))\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Thus, by
section 3
with
r
replaced by
\frac{r}{N}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{1}{3}}
, we have for
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\coloneqq\frac{r}{N}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{1}{3}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{1}}\leq\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2},\frac{1}{2}}}{h}_{1},

where
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,\Lambda)
, as the constant
\delta_{0}
depends only on
n,\Lambda
. Since we want to get the lower bound for each time level, we now apply the same argument to the function
\widetilde{h}_{1}(z)\coloneqq h_{1}(t_{0}+t,x,v)
for any
t_{1}\in[-(\frac{r}{4})^{2},0)
. More precisely, we observe that (
4.15
) also holds with
h_{1}
replaced by
\widetilde{h}_{1}
and that(
4.16
) holds with
h_{1}
,
A
and
B
replaced by
\widetilde{h}_{1}
,
A(t_{0}+t,x,v)
and
B(t_{0}+t,x,v)
. Thus, we deduce

(4.17)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{1}}\leq\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2},\frac{1}{2}}(t_{0},0,0)}{h}_{1}

for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,\Lambda)
and any
t_{0}\in[-(\frac{r}{4})^{2},0]
.

Step 2.
In this step, we consider the weak solution
h_{2}
of the corresponding equation with frozen coefficients

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h_{2}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h_{2}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A(0)\nabla_{v}h_{2})&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{4},\frac{r}{4N}}$},\\
h_{2}&=c_{1}h_{1}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{4},\frac{r}{4N}}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where the constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,\Lambda)
is given in (
4.17
). The goal of this step is to derive a suitable lower bound for
h_{2}
(see (
4.21
)). Since
h_{2}
solves a translation-invariant equation, we can apply the Hopf-type lemma from the previous subsection (see
subsection 4.1
).

First, we note
(t_{0},0,(\frac{r}{4N})^{3},0)\in H^{+}_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2},\frac{1}{2}}(t_{0},0,0)
for any
t_{0}\in[-(\frac{r}{8})^{2},0]
as
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}=\frac{r}{N}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{1}{3}}\in[\frac{r}{2N},\frac{r}{N}]
by
a_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Thus, we see

\displaystyle h_{2}(t_{0},0,(\frac{r}{4N})^{3},0)=c_{1}h_{1}(t_{0},0,(\frac{r}{4N})^{3},0)\geq 1

by (
4.17
). Since
|h_{1}|\leq 1
, and the constant
c_{1}
depends only on
n,\Lambda
, we have
|h_{2}|\leq c
, hence by
[
54
]
,

(4.18)

(r/N)^{\beta}[h_{2}]_{C^{\beta}(H_{{r/(8N)}}(z_{1}))}\leq c

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, for any
z_{1}=(t_{1},0,(\frac{r}{4N})^{3},0)
with
t_{1}\in[-(\frac{r}{8})^{2},0]
. As in (
4.15
), for any
t_{1}\in[-(r/8)^{2},0]
, we have

(4.19)

\displaystyle h_{2}(t_{1},0,(r/(4N))^{3}(1-a_{1}),0)\geq\frac{1}{2}

for some constant
a_{1}=a_{1}(n,\Lambda)
. Let
M_{1}=M_{1}(1/\delta_{1})
be the constant determined in
section 3
with
F\equiv 0
,
\delta=\delta_{1}
and
\varsigma=\sigma
, where
\delta_{1}\coloneqq(1-a_{1})^{-\frac{1}{3}}-1
and the constant
\sigma=\sigma(n,\Lambda)\leq\frac{1}{4}
is given in
subsection 4.1
. By choosing
N\coloneqq 4M_{0}+2M_{1}
, which depends only on
n,\Lambda
by the fact that constants
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{1}
depend only on
n,\Lambda
, and using (
4.19
), we have

(4.20)

\displaystyle{h}_{2}(-(M_{1}\widetilde{\rho}_{1})^{2},0,(\widetilde{\rho}_{1})^{3},0)\geq\frac{1}{2},

where
\widetilde{\rho}_{1}\coloneqq\frac{r}{4N}(1-a_{1})^{\frac{1}{3}}
, as
-(M_{1}\widetilde{\rho}_{1})^{2}\in[-(\frac{r}{8})^{2},0]
. Note that
-(M_{1}\widetilde{\rho}_{1})^{2}
can be close to
(-r/8)^{2}
. This is the reason why we had to show the lower bound given in (
4.17
) for each time level
t_{0}\in[-(\frac{r}{4})^{2},0]
.

Therefore, by
section 3
with
r=\widetilde{\rho}_{1}
,
\delta=\delta_{1}
and
\varsigma=\sigma
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\leq\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}{2},\sigma}(-({\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}/{4})^{2},0,0)}h_{2}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
. Thus, using
subsection 4.1
, we have

(4.21)

\displaystyle\inf_{H_{\frac{\sigma\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}{4}}}\frac{h_{2}}{{\varphi_{0}}}\geq\frac{1}{c}\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}{2},\sigma}(-({\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}/{4})^{2},0,0)}\frac{h_{2}}{{\varphi_{0}}}\geq\frac{1}{cr^{\frac{1}{2}}}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
, by (
4.1
) and since the constants
N
,
\sigma
and
a_{1}
depend only on
n,\Lambda
.

Step 3.
The goal of this step is to prove a lower bound of
h_{1}
via a perturbation argument and using the result from
Step 2
. In this step, for the convenience of the reader, we will say that a constant depends only on the given data, if it depends only on
n,\epsilon,\Lambda,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}
.

First, we use higher order expansions given in
subsection 2.2
to see that there are two constants
C_{1},C_{2}
such that for any
\varrho\leq\frac{r}{8N}
,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\|h_{1}-C_{1}{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\varrho})}+\|c_{1}^{-1}h_{2}-C_{2}{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\varrho})}\leq c\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon},

as
|h_{1}|+|h_{2}|\leq c(n,\Lambda)
and the constant
N
determined in (
4.20
) depends only on
n,\Lambda
. The main idea of the proof is to use the fact that the expansions in (
4.22
) are of higher order than
1/2
in order to deduce the lower bound for
h_{1}
from the one of
h_{2}
.

Claim 3.1.
We are going to prove that for any
\delta>0
, there is a sufficiently small
R_{0}
depending only on the given data and
\delta
such that if
r\leq R_{0}
, then

(4.23)

\displaystyle|C_{1}-C_{2}|\leq{\delta}{{r}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}.

This is the most delicate part of the proof. To show it, we observe that
h\coloneqq h_{1}-c_{1}^{-1}h_{2}
solves

(4.24)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A(0)\nabla_{v}h)&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{1}-\mathrm{div}_{v}((A(0)-A)\nabla_{v}h_{1})&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{4},\frac{r}{4N}}$},\\
h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{4},\frac{r}{4N}}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

First, using Sobolev’s inequality and using energy inequalities by testing
h
as in
[
37
, Lemma 4.6]
, we deduce

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{4},\frac{r}{4N}}}|h|^{2}\,dz\leq c{r}^{2}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{4},\frac{r}{4N}}}|\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\,dz\leq c{r}^{2\epsilon+2}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{2},\frac{r}{2N}}}|\nabla_{v}h_{1}|^{2}\,dz,

where we have also used the fact that
\|A-A(0)\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{{r}})}\leq c{r}^{\epsilon}
. On the other hand, by testing the equation (
4.13
) for
h_{1}
with
h_{1}\psi_{0}
, where
\psi_{0}\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{2},\frac{r}{2N}}
and
\psi_{0}\equiv 1
on
\mathcal{H}_{\frac{3r}{4},\frac{3r}{4N}}
, we derive

\displaystyle r^{2}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\frac{r}{2},\frac{r}{2N}}}|\nabla_{v}h_{1}|^{2}\,dz\leq c{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{\frac{3r}{4},\frac{3r}{4N}}}|h_{1}|^{2}\,dz\leq c.

Here we have also used the fact that
|h_{1}|\leq 1
. Therefore, altogether, we have proved

(4.25)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{I_{\frac{r}{4}}\times\mathrm{H}_{\frac{r}{4N}}}|h|^{2}\,dz\leq cr^{2\epsilon}.

Next, by applying
subsection 2.2
to the function
h_{1}
and using that
|h_{1}|\leq 1
, we have for any
z_{1}\in H_{\frac{r}{4N}}
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{v}h_{1}(z_{1})|\leq c\max\{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{1,n}|\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}r^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Thus, we are able to apply
subsection 4.2
with
a=\frac{4(1-\epsilon)}{1+\epsilon}<4
,
G_{1}=B\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{1}
and
G_{2}=(A-A(0))\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{1}
to (
4.24
) so that we deduce for any
\varrho\leq\frac{r}{8N}
,

\displaystyle|h(0,0,\varrho^{3},0)|

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r/N}}|h|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+cr^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}r^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}\right)\leq cr^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}

where we have used the fact that
\|A-A(0)\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})}\leq cr^{\epsilon}
and (
4.25
).

Therefore, we have for any
\varrho\leq\frac{r}{8N}
and
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1})=(0,0,\varrho^{3},0)
,

\displaystyle|(C_{1}-C_{2}){\varphi_{0}}(z_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq|(h_{1}-C_{1}{\varphi_{0}})(z_{1})|+|(c_{1}^{-1}h_{2}-C_{2}{\varphi_{0}})(z_{1})|+|h(z_{1})|\leq c\left(\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}+\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}r^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right).

Using the fact that
{\varphi_{0}}(z_{1})\eqsim|\varrho|^{\frac{1}{2}}
by (
4.1
), we deduce for any
\varrho\leq\frac{r}{8N}

(4.26)

\displaystyle|C_{1}-C_{2}|\leq c\varrho^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}+\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}r^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\leq cr^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}+r^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(\frac{r}{\varrho}\right)^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right).

By choosing
\varrho=\widetilde{\varrho}r
for some small
\widetilde{\varrho}>0
that depends only on the given data and
\delta
, we get

(4.27)

\displaystyle c\left(\frac{\varrho}{r}\right)^{\epsilon}=\frac{\delta}{2}.

Next, we choose a small constant
R_{0}
depending only on the given data and
\delta
to show that for any
r\leq R_{0}
, we have

(4.28)

\displaystyle cr^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\left(\frac{r}{\varrho}\right)^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}.

Plugging (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) into (
4.26
) yields (
4.23
) and therefore we have proved Claim 3.1.

Now we are ready to prove a lower bound of
h_{1}
.
By using (
4.21
) and (
4.22
), we derive

\displaystyle h_{1}(z)=c_{1}^{-1}h_{2}(z)-(c_{1}^{-1}h_{2}-h_{1})(z)\geq\frac{{\varphi_{0}}(z)}{cr^{\frac{1}{2}}}-c\frac{|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}}{r^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}}-(C_{1}-C_{2}){\varphi_{0}}(z)\quad\text{in }H_{\frac{\sigma\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}{4}},

where the constants
C_{i}
are determined in (
4.22
) and
\widetilde{\rho}_{1}=\frac{r}{4N}(1-a_{1})^{\frac{1}{3}}
with
a_{1}=a_{1}(n,\Lambda)\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

There is a small constant
\epsilon_{1}
depending only on the data so that we have

(4.29)

\displaystyle h_{1}(z)\geq\frac{{\varphi_{0}}(z)}{cr^{\frac{1}{2}}}-\frac{{\varphi_{0}}(z)}{2cr^{\frac{1}{2}}}-(C_{1}-C_{2}){\varphi_{0}}(z)\quad\text{for any }z\in H_{\epsilon_{1}\frac{\sigma\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-},

where we have used the second property given in (
4.1
). Next, note from (
4.23
), that there is a small constant
R_{0}
depending only on the given data so that if we choose
r\coloneqq R_{0}
, then

(4.30)

\displaystyle h_{1}(z)\geq\frac{{\varphi_{0}}(z)}{4cr^{\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\text{for any }z\in H_{\epsilon_{1}\frac{\sigma\widetilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}.

Step 4.
Now, we are in a position to conclude the proof. Using (
4.30
) and (
4.14
), we obtain

(4.31)

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}{\varphi_{0}}\quad\text{ in }H_{\frac{1}{C}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}.

Note that this would already prove the desired estimate, however, at this stage, the radius
1/C
of the cylinder still depends on the data through
A,B
.

In order to replace
H_{\frac{1}{C}}
in (
4.31
) by
\rho
, we first apply all the previous steps of the proof to suitable translations of
f
in
t
. To be precise, given
t_{0}\in[-(\rho/8)^{2},0]
we denote
{f}_{z_{0}}(z)\coloneqq f(z_{0}\circ z)
where we write
z_{0}=(t_{0},0,0)
. Then, we observe that by (
4.12
), we get for any
\varsigma\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
,

(4.32)

\displaystyle{f}_{z_{0}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}\quad\text{on }H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},{2\varsigma}}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma)
, where
\rho
is universally determined at the first line of the proof.

Therefore, by repeating all the previous arguments for
{f}_{z_{0}}
instead of
f
, and using that
{\varphi_{0}}\geq\frac{1}{c}
in
H_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma}^{+}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma}
, we get

(4.33)

\displaystyle f_{z_{0}}(z)\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}{\varphi_{0}}(z)\quad\text{in }H_{\frac{1}{C}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}

for any
t_{0}\in[-(\frac{\rho}{8})^{2},0,0]
. In particular, we have used (
4.32
) with
\varsigma=\frac{2r}{N\rho}
so that the constant
c
given in (
4.32
) depends only on the given data.

In order to complete the proof, it remains to derive a lower bound of
f_{z_{0}}
in the set

\displaystyle S\coloneqq\big((I_{\frac{1}{C}}\times\mathrm{H}_{\frac{\rho}{8}})\setminus H_{\frac{1}{C}}\big)\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}.

To this end, we apply (
4.32
) with
\varsigma=\frac{1}{C}
, which yields

\displaystyle f_{z_{0}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}\quad\text{on }H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{4},\frac{2}{C}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},{\frac{2}{C}}},

where the constant
c
depends only on the given data, as the constant
C
depends on the data. Since
S\subset H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{4},\frac{2}{C}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},{\frac{2}{C}}}
and
{\varphi_{0}}\leq c
in
S
for some constant
c
depending only on the data, we have
f_{z_{0}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}{\varphi_{0}}
in
S
.
By combination of this estimate with (
4.33
), we have shown altogether that

\displaystyle f_{z_{0}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}{\varphi_{0}}\quad\text{in }(I_{\frac{1}{C}}\times\mathrm{H}_{\frac{\rho}{8}})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}.

Since we have defined
f_{z_{0}}(z)=f(z_{0}\circ z)
with
z_{0}=(t_{0},0,0)
and
t_{0}\in[-(\rho/8)^{2},0]
, we conclude

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}{\varphi_{0}}\quad\text{in }{H}_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-},

which completes the proof.
∎

Now we give the proof of the main result of this subsection. Due to
subsection 4.2
, it only remains to generalize the result to the case
F\not\equiv 0
. We achieve this by applying the maximum principle.

Proof of
Theorem 4.3
.

First, fix a small constant
\varsigma_{0}<\frac{1}{4}
, which will be determined later (see (
4.38
)). We now fix a large universal constant
M
and a large constant
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(n,\Lambda,\varsigma_{0})
determined in
section 3
applied with
\varsigma=\varsigma_{0}
and

\delta=1
. Then we fix
\rho\coloneqq\frac{1}{M}
. Then by
section 3
with
r=\rho
, we have for
\mathcal{M}\geq\mathcal{M}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma_{0}}}f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma_{0})
. Moreover, by assuming
\mathcal{M}\geq\mathcal{M}_{1}
for some constant
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}_{1}(n,\Lambda)
, we deduce from
section 3
with
a=0
and
r=\frac{\rho}{4}
that
f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma_{0}}
,
where the set
\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma_{0}}
is given in (
3.25
).
Now we assume
\mathcal{M}\geq\mathcal{M}_{0}+\mathcal{M}_{1}
to see that

(4.34)

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}\quad\text{in }H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma_{0}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma_{0}}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma_{0})
.

Next, we consider a weak solution
h_{1}
of

(4.35)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h_{1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h_{1}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}h_{1})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}h_{1}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}$},\\
h_{1}&=1&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Gamma_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}$},\\
h_{1}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{H}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}\setminus\Gamma_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where the sets
\mathcal{H}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}
and
\Gamma_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}
are defined in (
4.2
).
Since
\frac{c_{0}}{c}h_{1}\leq f
on
\Gamma_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}\subset H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\varsigma_{0}}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2\varsigma_{0}}
due to (
4.34
), by the maximum principle, we have

(4.36)

\displaystyle h_{1}\leq\frac{c}{c_{0}}f\quad\text{in }\mathcal{H}_{\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\varsigma_{0})
.

Now we will choose a constant
\varsigma_{0}
to apply
subsection 4.2
to an appropriately rescaled version of
h_{1}
. The argument is close to
Step 1
in the proof of
subsection 4.2
. As in (
4.15
), there is a small constant
a_{0}=a_{0}(n,\Lambda)\leq\frac{1}{2}
such that
for any
z_{1}=(t_{1},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1,n},v_{1})=(t_{1},0,(\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2})^{3},0)
with
t_{1}\in[-(\rho/4)^{2},0]
,

(4.37)

\displaystyle h_{1}(t_{1},0,({\varsigma_{0}\rho}/{2})^{3}(1-a_{0}),0)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Let
M_{1}=M_{1}(1/\delta_{0})
be the constant determined in
section 3
, where
\delta_{0}\coloneqq(1-a_{0})^{-\frac{1}{3}}-1
.
Now we choose

(4.38)

\varsigma_{0}\coloneqq\frac{(1-a_{0})^{-\frac{1}{3}}}{2M_{1}},

which gives
{h}_{1}(-(\widetilde{\rho}_{0}M_{1})^{2},0,(\widetilde{\rho}_{0})^{3},0)\geq\frac{1}{2}
with
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}\coloneqq\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{1}{3}}
, by (
4.37
). Note that the constant
\varsigma_{0}
depends only on
n,\Lambda
, as
a_{0}
and
\delta_{0}
depend only on
n,\Lambda
. Therefore, applying
section 3
with
r=\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
,
\delta=\delta_{0}
and
\varsigma=\frac{1}{4M}
,
we have

(4.39)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{1}}\leq\inf_{H^{+}_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2},\frac{1}{4M}}}{h}_{1},

where
M
is the universal constant determined in
section 3
with
\delta=1
and
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,\Lambda)
. Therefore, we define
\overline{h}_{1}(z)\coloneqq h_{1}(S_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2}}z)
to see that

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\overline{h}_{1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\overline{h}_{1}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(\overline{A}\nabla_{v}\overline{h}_{1})&=\overline{B}\cdot\nabla_{v}\overline{h}_{1}&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{4}$},\\
\overline{h}_{1}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{4}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\overline{A}(z)\coloneqq A(S_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2}}z)
,
\overline{B}(z)\coloneqq B(S_{\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2}}z)
. Note from the first lines in the proof of
subsection 4.2
that the constant
\rho=\frac{1}{M}
is the same one as in
subsection 4.2
. Moreover, as
-(\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2})^{2}=-(\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{8})^{2}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{2}{3}}\geq(-\frac{\rho}{8})^{2}
and
\frac{\rho\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2}\in[\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{8M},\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}}{2}]
, we have
\overline{h}_{1}(-1,0,\rho^{3},0)\in[\frac{1}{c_{1}},1]
by (
4.39
). Therefore, by
subsection 4.2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\leq\inf_{H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}}\frac{\overline{h}_{1}}{{\varphi_{0}}}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{1})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})})
. A combination of this and (
4.36
) proves the following fact: There is
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,\Lambda)
, such that whenever
\mathcal{M}\geq C_{0}
, then it holds

(4.40)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\leq\inf_{H_{a\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}}\frac{f}{{\varphi_{0}}}

for some constants
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{1})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})})
and
a=a(n,\Lambda)\in(0,1)
. Here, we recall that
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}=\frac{\varsigma_{0}\rho}{2}(1-a_{0})^{\frac{1}{3}}
and that constants
a_{0}
and
\varsigma_{0}
depend only on
n,\Lambda
. In particular, the constants
a_{0}
and
\varsigma_{0}
are determined in (
4.37
) and (
4.38
), respectively.

It remains to obtain the estimate (
4.40
) with
\frac{\rho}{8}
instead of
a\rho
.

To this end, we need to prove a lower bound of
f
in
(H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus H_{a\rho})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
. First note that the constant
a=a(n,\Lambda)
is fixed and depends only on
n,\Lambda
. Using this, as in (
4.34
), there is a large constant
\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathcal{M}_{2}(n,\Lambda)
such that if
\mathcal{M}\geq\mathcal{M}_{2}
, then

(4.41)

\displaystyle f\geq\frac{c_{0}}{c}\quad\text{in }H^{+}_{\frac{\rho}{2},a}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\frac{\rho}{4},2a}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda)
. Now we fix
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq C_{0}+\mathcal{M}_{2}
, which depends only on
n,\Lambda
. Therefore, using (
4.40
) and (
4.41
) together with the fact that
{\varphi_{0}}\eqsim c(n,\Lambda)
in
(H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus H_{a\rho})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
by (
4.1
), we derive

(4.42)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{c}\leq\inf_{(I_{a\rho}\times\mathrm{H}_{\frac{\rho}{8}})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}}\frac{f}{{\varphi_{0}}}.

As in the last part given in
subsection 4.2
, by showing (
4.42
) for each time level
t\in[-(\frac{\rho}{8})^{2},0]
, we deduce the desired estimate. This completes the proof.

∎

Next we provide the following auxiliary result which was used in the proof of
subsection 4.2
. It shows expansions of order
\frac{a}{4+a}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
at
\gamma_{0}
for solutions to equations with source terms that explode mildly at
\gamma_{0}
.
The proof follows from
subsection 2.2
by a perturbation argument.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
A\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})
with
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
r\in(0,1]
. Let
f
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}{f})&=G_{0}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G_{1})&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{r}$},\\
{f}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
G_{i}
satisfy

(4.43)

\displaystyle|G_{i}(z_{1})|\leq c_{i}\max\{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{1,n}|\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

for some
c_{i}\geq 0
. Then for any
a\in(0,4)
and

\rho\in(0,\frac{r}{2}]
, we have

\displaystyle|f(0,0,\rho^{3},0)|\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+c_{0}r^{\frac{a}{4+a}+1}+c_{1}r^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},a)
.

Proof.

First, we observe from (
4.43
) that

(4.44)

\displaystyle\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|G_{i}|^{4+a}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{a+4}}\leq cc_{0}r^{-\frac{4}{a+4}}\left(\int_{\mathrm{H}_{1}}\max\{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{1,n}|\}^{-\frac{a+4}{2}}\,dx_{n}\,dv_{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{a+4}}\leq cc_{i}r^{-\frac{4}{a+4}}

for some constant
c=c(a)
.
First, we are going to prove that for any
z_{1}=(0,0,x_{1,n},0)\in H_{r/2}
and
\rho\leq r/2
,

(4.45)

\displaystyle E(f;H_{\rho}(z_{1}))\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r+c_{1})r^{\frac{a}{a+4}}\right),

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},a)
and we write

\displaystyle E(f;H_{\rho}(z_{1}))\coloneqq\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\rho}(z_{1})}|f-(f)_{H_{\rho}(z_{1})}|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

To this end, we divide the proof into two cases. Within this proof, all constants
c
depend only on
n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{r})},a
.

•

Let
z_{1}=0
. Let
h
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h+v\cdot\nabla_{x}h-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}{h})&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{r/4}$},\\
{h}&=f&&\qquad\mbox{on $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then, by using the Poincaré inequality and testing the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(f-h)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}(f-h)-\mathrm{div}_{v}({A}\nabla_{v}{(f-h)})=G_{0}+\mathrm{div}_{v}(G_{1})\quad\text{in }H_{\frac{r}{4}},

with
h-f
, we deduce

\displaystyle J\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}|h-f|^{2}\,dz\leq c{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}r^{2}|\nabla_{v}(h-f)|^{2}\,dz\leq cr^{2}\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}|r\nabla_{v}(h-f)|^{2}+{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}|rG_{0}|^{2}+|G_{1}|^{2}\,dz\right).

Moreover, note that by testing the equation for
h
with
h-f
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}r^{2}|\nabla_{v}h|^{2}\,dz\leq c{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}r^{2}|\nabla_{v}f|^{2}\,dz.

Next, we use energy estimates of
f
, the Hölder inequality and the observation (
4.44
) so that we further estimate
J
as

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle\leq cr^{2}\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|rG_{0}|^{2}+|G_{1}|^{2}\,dz\right)

\displaystyle\leq cr^{2}\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|rG_{0}|^{4+a}\,dz\right)^{\frac{2}{a+4}}+\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|G_{1}|^{4+a}\,dz\right)^{\frac{2}{a+4}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq cr^{2}\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+c_{1}^{2}r^{2-\frac{8}{a+4}}+c_{0}^{2}r^{-\frac{8}{a+4}}\right).

Next, as in (
2.13
), from
subsection 2.2
, we deduce for any
\sigma\in(0,\frac{1}{16}]
,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{{\sigma r}}}|h|^{2}\leq c\sigma{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{4}}}|h|^{2}\,dz.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\sigma r}}|f|^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\leq c\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\sigma r}}|h|^{2}\,dz+\sigma^{-(4n+2)}J\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\sigma\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+(1+\sigma)\sigma^{-(4n+2)}J\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\sigma\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+\sigma^{-(4n+2)}r^{2}\left[{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+c_{0}^{2}r^{2-\frac{8}{a+4}}+c_{1}^{2}r^{-\frac{8}{a+4}}\right]\right).

We can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\int_{H_{\sigma r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\leq c\left((\sigma^{4n+3}+r^{2})\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+(c_{0}^{2}r^{2}+c_{1}^{2})r^{4n+2+\frac{2a}{a+4}}\right).

Thus, if
r\leq r_{0}
for some small
r_{0}
, then by
[
27
, Lemma 3.4]
, we deduce for any
\rho\leq r
,

\displaystyle\int_{H_{\rho}}|f|^{2}\,dz\leq c\left(\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{4n+2+\frac{2a}{a+4}}\int_{H_{\frac{r}{2}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+(c_{0}^{2}r^{2}+c_{1}^{2})\rho^{4n+2+\frac{2a}{a+4}}\right).

Here, we also used that
4n+2+\frac{2a}{a+4}\leq 4n+3
since
a<4
.

Thus we have for any
\rho\leq r\leq r_{0}
,

(4.46)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\rho}}|f|^{2}\,dz\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{2a}{a+4}}{\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{{r}}}|f|^{2}\,dz+(c_{0}^{2}r^{2}+c_{1}^{2})\rho^{\frac{2a}{a+4}}.

When
r>r_{0}
, then
r\in(r_{0},1]
. Thus, we have for any
\rho\leq r_{0}

\displaystyle\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{\rho}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r_{0}}\right)^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r_{0}}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r_{0}+c_{1})r_{0}^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r+c_{1})r^{\frac{a}{4+a}}\right),

as
r\in(r_{0},1]
and the constant
r_{0}
depends only on the given data. Moreover, when
\rho\in(r_{0},r/2]
, we directly obtain the above estimates, as
(\rho/r)\eqsim 1
. Therefore, (
4.45
) follows from (
4.46
).

•

Let
z_{1}=(0,0,x_{1,n},0)
with
x_{1,n}\neq 0
. Let
r_{1}\coloneqq\frac{(x_{1,n})^{\frac{1}{3}}}{4}
to see that

(4.47)

\displaystyle H_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\cap\{x_{n}>0\}=\emptyset\quad\text{and}\quad H_{r_{1}}(z_{1})\subset H_{8r_{1}}.

Suppose
\rho\leq r_{1}/2
. Then by
subsection 2.2
,
L^{\infty}-L^{2}
estimates by
[
54
, Theorem 1.1]
and (
4.43
),

\displaystyle r_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}[f]_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}(H_{r_{1}/2}(z_{1}))}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{3r_{1}/4}(z_{1}))}+r_{1}^{2}\|G_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{3r_{1}/4}(z_{1}))}+r_{1}\|G_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{3r_{1}/4}(z_{1}))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r_{1}}(z_{1})}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r_{1}+c_{1})r_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),

which implies

\displaystyle E(f;H_{\rho}(z_{1}))\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r_{1}}(z_{1})}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r_{1}+c_{1})r_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

By (
4.47
) and (
4.46
), we have

\displaystyle E(f;H_{\rho}(z_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{8r_{1}}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r_{1}+c_{1})r_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{a}{a+4}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r+c_{1})r^{\frac{a}{a+4}}\right).

On the other hand, when
\rho\geq r_{1}/2
, we can directly use (
4.46
) to see that by
H_{\rho}(z_{1})\subset H_{8\rho}
,

\displaystyle E(f;H_{\rho}(z_{1}))\leq c\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{a}{a+4}}\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r+c_{1})r^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

We have verified (
4.45
). Thus, we have for any
z_{1}=(0,0,\rho^{3},0)
,

\displaystyle|f(z_{1})|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|(f)_{H_{2^{-i}\rho}(z_{1})}-(f)_{H_{2^{-(i+1)}\rho}(z_{1})}|+|(f)_{H_{\rho/2}(z_{1})}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}E(f;H_{2^{-i}\rho}(z_{1}))+c|(f)_{H_{4\rho}}|.

where we have used that
H_{\rho/2}(z_{1})\subset H_{4\rho}
. Therefore, a combination of this, (
4.45
) and (
4.46
) leads to

\displaystyle|f(z_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-i\left(\frac{a}{a+4}\right)}+1\right)\left(\left({\mathchoice{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-9.7pt}{\kern 4.30554pt\vbox{\hrule height=0.4pt,width=4.3pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-7.0pt}{}{}}\int_{H_{r}}|f|^{2}\,dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(c_{0}r+c_{1})r^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

This completes the proof.
∎

4.3.
Hopf lemma in non-flat domains

We end this section with the proof of the Hopf lemma in a general curved domain (see
Theorem 1.5
).

Proof of
Theorem 1.5
.

By
[
38
, Section 8]
, there is a
C^{k+\epsilon}
diffeomorphism
\varphi^{-1}
such that for
\Psi(t,y,w)\coloneqq(t,\varphi^{-1}(y),\nabla\varphi^{-1}(y)w)
,

\displaystyle I_{2}\times\mathrm{H}_{{R_{1}}}\subset\Psi(I_{2}\times(\Omega\cap B_{R_{0}^{3}})\times B_{R_{0}})\subset I_{2}\times\mathrm{H}_{2R_{1}}

for some constants
R_{0},R_{1}\leq 1
depending only on
\Omega
.
In particular,
\widetilde{f}\coloneqq f\circ\Psi^{-1}
is a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\widetilde{f}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{f}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(\widetilde{A}\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f})&=\widetilde{B}\cdot\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f}+\widetilde{F}&&\qquad\mbox{in $I_{2}\times\mathrm{H}_{2R_{1}}$},\\
\widetilde{f}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\widetilde{A}
,
\widetilde{B}
, and
\widetilde{F}
are defined as in
[
38
, (8.3)]
. In particular, by
[
53
, Lemma 2.17]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{A}\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{R_{1}})}\leq c\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(\mathbf{H}_{2}(z_{0}))},\quad\|\widetilde{B}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R_{1}})}\leq c\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{H}_{2}(z_{0}))},\quad\|\widetilde{F}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R_{1}})}\leq\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{H}_{2}(z_{0}))},

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\Omega)
. In addition, using the scaling argument from
[
38
, Proposition 5.1]
, by considering
\widetilde{f}_{a}(z)=\widetilde{f}(t,ax,av)
for
a\coloneqq\sqrt{(\widetilde{A}(0))_{n,n}}
, we can assume
(\widetilde{A}(0))_{n,n}=1
. Moreover, we now choose a constant
\rho_{1}=\rho_{1}(\Omega)
such that
\Psi(-1,x_{0}-\rho_{1}^{3}n_{x_{0}},0)=(-1,0,(\rho R_{1})^{3},0)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\rho
is the universal constant determined in
Theorem 5.1
. Next, by choosing a sufficiently small constant
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda,\Omega)
and using the Harnack chain argument as in (
3.27
) with
v_{1}=0
, we obtain
\widetilde{f}(-(R_{1})^{2},0,(\rho R_{1})^{3},0)=\frac{c_{0}}{c}
for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\Omega)
. Now we apply a rescaled version of
Theorem 4.3
to the function
\widetilde{f}
to get

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{c}\leq\inf_{H_{\frac{\rho R_{1}}{8}}}\frac{\widetilde{f}}{{\varphi_{0}}}

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|\widetilde{A}\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{R_{1}})},\|\widetilde{B}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R_{1}})})
. Now, using the fact that
\Psi^{-1}(\{x_{n}\leq(v_{n})^{3}\})\subset\mathcal{R}_{-}
,
{\varphi_{0}}(\Psi(z))={\varphi_{0}}(d_{\Omega}(x),n_{x}\cdot v)
by
[
38
, (8.4)]
, scaling back and (
2.9
), we get the desired result. Since we have considered the function
\widetilde{f}_{a}(z)(=\widetilde{f}(t,ax,av))
to assume
(\widetilde{A}(0))_{n,n}=1
, the constant
\rho_{1}
eventually depends on
\Omega,\Lambda
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Higher order boundary Harnack estimates

In this section, we will prove the following boundary Harnack principle.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
k\in\{0,1\}

\epsilon\in(0,1)\setminus\{\frac{1}{2}\}
with
k+\epsilon<\frac{3}{2}
and let
A\in C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{2})
,
B\in C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{2})

F_{i}\in C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{2})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
f_{i}
be weak solutions to

(5.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f_{i}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{i}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f_{i})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{i}+F_{i}&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{2}$},\\
f_{i}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{2}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

with
f_{2},F_{2}\geq 0
, and
(A(0))_{n,n}=1
. Suppose
\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})},\|F_{i}\|_{C^{\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{2})}\leq 1
. There exists a universal constant
\rho<1
and a large constant
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(n,\Lambda)
such that if
f_{2}(z_{0})=c_{0}>0
for some constant
c_{0}
with
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},x_{0,n},v_{0})=(-1,0,\rho^{3},0)
and

\displaystyle\|F_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{2})}\leq\frac{c_{0}}{\mathcal{M}},

then

\displaystyle\left[\frac{f_{1}}{f_{2}}\right]_{C^{\min\{1+\epsilon,3/2\}}(H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq c,

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,c_{0},\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{C^{\epsilon-1}(H_{2})})
.
Moreover, when
F_{i}\equiv 0
, we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{f_{1}}{f_{2}}\right]_{C^{1,\max\{\epsilon,k\}}(H_{\frac{\rho}{8}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq c

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,c_{0},\|A\|_{C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{2})},\|B\|_{C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{2})})
, where
\mathcal{R}_{-}=\{x_{n}\leq v_{n}^{3}\}
.

To obtain this result, the key step is to prove an expansion of
f_{1}/f_{2}
at
\gamma_{0}
(see
subsection 5.2
) under the assumption that
{\varphi_{0}}\lesssim f_{2}
in
H_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
. Afterwards, we combine this expansion with regularity estimates away from
\gamma_{0}\cup\gamma_{-}
(i.e. interior estimates and boundary estimates up to
\gamma_{+}
) to derive uniform regularity estimates in
\mathcal{R}_{+}\cup\mathcal{R}_{0}
(see
subsection 5.3
). Finally, we employ
Theorem 4.3
to replace the assumption
{\varphi_{0}}\lesssim f_{2}
in
H_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
by the assumption that
f(z_{0})=c_{0}>0
.

5.1.
Auxiliary results

In this section, we provide several auxiliary lemmas that will be used for the proof of the main result.

We first prove the following fact about the functions
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3}
, which are determined in (
2.9
):

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume
a^{n,n}=1
. Then, for any function
\varphi\in\mathrm{span}\{{\varphi_{0}},v_{i}\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},t\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},v_{i}v_{j}\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}\}
, there is a function
\phi\in\mathrm{span}\{{\varphi_{3}},v_{i}v_{j}{\varphi_{0}},v_{i}v_{j}{\varphi_{1}},t{\varphi_{0}},t{\varphi_{1}}\}
such that

(5.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\phi+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}\phi&=\varphi&&\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\phi&=0&&\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ on }\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}=0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{aligned}\right.

Proof.

We deduce from (
2.4
), (
2.6
), (
2.9
), and since
a^{n,n}=1
that

\displaystyle v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}(v_{n}^{2}{\varphi_{0}})-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}(v_{n}^{2}{\varphi_{0}})

\displaystyle=-2{\varphi_{0}}-4{\varphi_{1}},

\displaystyle v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}(v_{n}^{2}{\varphi_{1}})-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}(v_{n}^{2}{\varphi_{1}})

\displaystyle=-6{\varphi_{1}}-7v_{n}^{2}\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(t{\varphi_{1}})+v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}(t{\varphi_{1}})-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}(t{\varphi_{1}})

\displaystyle={\varphi_{1}}-3t\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},

\displaystyle v\cdot\nabla_{x}(v_{l}v_{k}{\varphi_{0}})-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}(v_{l}v_{k}{\varphi_{0}})

\displaystyle=-2a^{l,k}{\varphi_{0}}-2(a^{l,n}v_{k}+a^{k,n}v_{l})\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},

\displaystyle v\cdot\nabla_{x}(v_{l}v_{k}{\varphi_{1}})-a^{i,j}\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}(v_{l}v_{k}{\varphi_{1}})

\displaystyle=-3v_{l}v_{k}\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}-2a^{l,k}{\varphi_{1}}

\displaystyle-2(a^{l,n}v_{k}+a^{k,n}v_{l})(v_{n}\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}+\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}).

By the aforementioned observations and (
2.5
), we immediately deduce the desired result.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

For
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\eqsim_{n}\sum_{i=0}^{3}\|p_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}.

In addition, if
{\varphi_{0}}/c_{2}\leq f\leq c_{2}{\varphi_{0}}
in
H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
for some constant
c_{2}\geq 1
and
p_{0}(0)=p_{1}(0)=0
, then for any
r\leq 1
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.5)

\displaystyle\left\|af+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\eqsim_{n,c_{2}}r^{\frac{1}{2}}|a|+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\|r^{\alpha_{i}}p_{i}(S_{r}z)\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})},

where we write

(5.6)

\mbox{$\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}=\frac{1}{2}$, $\alpha_{2}=2$ and $\alpha_{3}=\frac{5}{2}$}.

Proof.

The first relation (
5.4
) follows from the fact that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\leq\|{\varphi_{i}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq c

for some constant
c\geq 1
, and
\{{\varphi_{i}}\}_{i}
is a linearly independent set. For (
5.5
), first we prove

(5.8)

\displaystyle\left\|af+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\geq\frac{1}{c}\left(|a|+\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,c_{2})
.
First, we assume
M|a|\leq\left\|\sum\limits_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}

for some constant
M=2c_{2}\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\|af+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\geq\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}-\|af\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}.

Thus, we assume
M|a|\geq\left\|\sum\limits_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}
, which implies
\|p_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}\leq c(n)M|a|
by (
5.4
). Note from (
2.7
) and the fact that
p_{0}(0)=p_{1}(0)=0
, that there is a small constant
\rho=\rho(n,c_{2})
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{3}\|p_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}\|\varphi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\leq c(n)M|a|\rho^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq\frac{|a|}{2c_{2}}\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})},

as the constant
M
depends only on
c_{2}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\left\|af+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}\geq\|af\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}-\left\|\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\|af\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}}{2}\geq\frac{|a|}{c}

for some constant
c=c(n,c_{2})
. Altogether, we obtain (
5.8
). Now, a combination of (
5.8
) and (
5.7
) leads to (
5.5
) with
r=1
. For any
r\in(0,1)
, we use the scaling argument and (
2.7
) to get that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left\|af+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}

\displaystyle=\left\|af(S_{r}z)+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}(S_{r}z){\varphi_{i}}(S_{r}z)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})}

\displaystyle=\left\|r^{\frac{1}{2}}af_{0}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}r^{\alpha_{i}}p_{i}(S_{r}z){\varphi_{i}}(z)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-})},

where we write
f_{0}(z)=f(S_{r}z)/r^{\frac{1}{2}}
and it satisfies
{\varphi_{0}}/c\leq f_{0}\leq c{\varphi_{0}}
in
H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
for some constant
c=c(c_{2})
. Thus, the desired estimate follows from applying (
5.5
) with
r=1
into the last term given in (
5.9
). This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. If
p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{3}}
solves

(5.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{3}})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}(p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{3}})-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}(p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{3}}))=0

for some uniformly elliptic constant coefficient matrix
A
with
(A)_{n,n}=1
, then

(5.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A)_{n,j}(\nabla_{v}p(0))_{j}=0.

In addition, if there is a vector
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
a sequence of uniformly elliptic constant coefficient matrices
(A_{i})_{i}
such that
A_{i}\to A
with
(A_{i})_{n,n}=1
and

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A)_{n,j}\mathbf{Q}_{j}=0,

then there is a sequence of vectors
(\mathbf{Q}_{i})_{i}
such that
\mathbf{Q}=\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{Q}_{i}
and

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A_{i})_{n,j}(\mathbf{Q}_{i})_{j}=0.

Proof.

Let us write
p_{2}(z)\coloneqq p(z)-(p(0)+\nabla_{v}p(0)\cdot v)
to see that
p_{2}(0)=\nabla_{v}p_{2}(0)=0
. Note that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{3}})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}(p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{1}})-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}(p{\varphi_{0}}+a_{0}{\varphi_{3}}))

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A)_{n,j}(\nabla_{v}p(0))_{j}\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A)_{n,j}\partial_{v_{j}}p_{2}\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}+c_{1}{\varphi_{0}}

for some constant
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, where we have used the fact that
v_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}-\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}=0
. Thus, by (
5.10
) and since all elements of
\{\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},v_{j}\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}},{\varphi_{0}}\}
are linearly independent, we obtain

\displaystyle-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A)_{n,j}(\nabla_{v}p(0))_{j}=-2\sum_{j=1}^{n}(A)_{n,j}\partial_{v_{j}}p_{2}=c_{1}=0,

which proves (
5.11
).

Next, suppose
(A)_{n}\cdot\mathbf{Q}=0
. Now choose
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=\mathbf{Q}+q_{n}e_{n}
, where
e_{n}
is a
n
-th unit normal vector and
q_{n}=((A)_{n}-(A_{i})_{n})\cdot\mathbf{Q}
. Then, we see that
(A_{i})_{n}\cdot\mathbf{Q}_{i}=0
and
\mathbf{Q}_{i}\to\mathbf{Q}
, as
(A_{i})_{n,n}=1
and
(A_{i})_{n}\to(A)_{n}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and let
A\in C^{\epsilon}(H_{1})
,
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{1})
. Let
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
. Let
f
be a weak solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G{\varphi_{i}})+g\partial_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{i}}\quad\text{in }H_{1}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}\leq c(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+\|G\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{1})}+[F]_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{1})}+\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})})

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{1})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})})
.

Proof.

Note that the functions
\varphi_{i}
are smooth inside
\{x_{n}>0\}
. Therefore, by following the proof of
[
38
, Lemma 5.3]
, we immediately derive the desired estimate.
∎

Moreover, from
[
38
, Lemma 5.2, Lemma 5.4]
, we deduce the following.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
A\in C^{\alpha}(H_{R})
and
B\in L^{\infty}(H_{R})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Let
f
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G)&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{R}$},\\
f&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap{Q}_{R}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
F\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i=0}^{2}F_{i}\partial^{(i)}_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}+F_{3}
,
G\coloneqq\sum\limits_{j=0}^{1}G_{j}\partial^{(i)}_{v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}}+G_{2}
with
F_{i}\in C^{\lambda_{i},\alpha^{\prime}}
,
G_{j}\in C^{\mu_{j},\alpha^{\prime}}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,1)
and
\lambda_{i},\mu_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
for each
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
and
j\in\{0,1,2\}
. Then there is a small constant
\beta=\beta(n,\Lambda,\alpha)
such that

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{\beta}(H_{R/2})}\leq c\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}[F_{i}]_{C^{\lambda_{i},\alpha^{\prime}}(H_{R})}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}[G_{j}]_{C^{\mu_{j},\alpha^{\prime}}(H_{R})}\right),

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\lambda_{i},\mu_{j},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\|A\|_{C^{\alpha}(H_{R})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})},R)
.

Proof.

The result follows immediately from
[
38
, Lemma 5.2, Lemma 5.4]
.
∎

Finally, we provide a Schauder type regularity result away from
\gamma_{-}\cup\gamma_{0}
. It follows directly from a combination of
subsection 2.2
and
subsection 2.2
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
k\in\{1,2\}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
A\in C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
,
B\in C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
,
F\in C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
, and
G\in C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))
with
z_{0}\notin\mathcal{R}_{-}
and
r\leq\frac{\mathrm{dist}(z_{0},\gamma_{0})}{4}
. Assume that
f
is a weak solution to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G)\quad\text{in }H_{r}(z_{0}).

Then we have

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(H_{r/2}(z_{0}))}\leq cr^{-(k+\epsilon)}\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{k+\epsilon}[F]_{C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))}+r^{k+\epsilon}[G]_{C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{k-1,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))},\|B\|_{C^{k-2,\epsilon}(H_{r}(z_{0}))})
.

Proof.

Since
H_{r}(z_{0})\cap(\gamma_{0}\cup\gamma_{-})=\emptyset
, combining
subsection 2.2
and
subsection 2.2
together with a standard covering argument yields the desired estimate.
∎

5.2.
Expansions at the grazing set

Now we are ready to prove expansion estimates in terms of two solutions
f_{1},f_{2}
of order less than three. Note that these expansions have a very specific form, and we collect all possible information about the prefactors within the expansion estimate. This will be crucial to deduce the boundary Harnack principle of optimal order in the sequel. A crucial observation is that the expansion does not involve the function
\varphi_{0}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
k\in\{0,1\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)\setminus\{\frac{1}{2}\}
with
k+\epsilon<\frac{3}{2}
. Let
A\in C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{1})
,
B\in C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{1})
,
F_{i}\in C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
f_{i}
be weak solutions to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f_{i}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{i}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f_{i})&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{i}+F_{i}&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{1}$},\\
f_{i}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{1}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
(A)_{n,n}(0)=1
. Suppose that
f_{2}\geq 0
and that there is
c_{2}\geq 1
such that

(5.12)

\frac{\varphi_{0}}{c_{2}}\leq f_{2}\quad\text{in }H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}.

Then there exist
b_{0},b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,q\in\mathcal{P}_{1+k}
with
q(0)=\nabla_{v}q(0)=0
, and

(5.13)

\displaystyle(A(0))_{n}\cdot\nabla_{v}p(0)=0,

such that for any
z\in H_{r}
with
r\leq\frac{1}{2}
,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\|f_{1}-pf_{2}-q\varphi_{1}-b_{0}{\varphi_{2}}-b_{1}{\varphi_{3}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})}\leq cr^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})}\right),

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{1})},\|B\|_{C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{1})},c_{2})
. Moreover, if
F_{i}\equiv 0
, then it holds
b_{0}=0
.

Proof.

In order to establish (
5.14
), we will prove that there exists
(a,p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{1+k}\times\mathcal{P}_{1+k}\times\mathcal{P}_{[k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}]}\times\mathcal{P}_{[k+\epsilon-1]}
such that

p_{0}(0)=p_{1}(0)=\nabla_{v}p_{1}(0)=0
,
(A(0))_{n}\cdot\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)=0
and

(5.15)

\displaystyle\left\|f_{1}-af_{2}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r})}\leq cr^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{1})},\|B\|_{C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{1})},c_{2})
.

First, let us prove that (
5.15
) implies the desired result (
5.14
). To this end, note that by
subsection 2.2
,

(5.16)

\displaystyle f_{2}=a_{0}{\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v){\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v){\varphi_{1}}+g,

where
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\displaystyle|g(z)|

\displaystyle\leq c|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+k+\epsilon}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{3}{2}}(H_{1})}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{1})},\|B\|_{C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{1})},c_{2})
with
k+\epsilon<\frac{3}{2}
. Moreover, we have
\mathbf{a}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{2}=0
when
k=0
, and
a_{0}>0
by (
5.12
).

Next, recalling that
a,p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
are determined in (
5.15
), we define polynomials
p
and
q
by

\displaystyle p\coloneqq a+\frac{(\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v)}{a_{0}}+\frac{v^{\mathrm{t}}Qv}{a_{0}}+\frac{\partial_{t}p_{0}}{a_{0}}t,

\displaystyle q\coloneqq p_{1}-(\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v)\frac{(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v)}{a_{0}},\quad b_{0}\coloneqq p_{2},\quad b_{1}\coloneqq p_{3},

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad v^{\mathrm{t}}Qv=v^{\mathrm{t}}\nabla_{v}^{2}p_{0}v-(\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v)\frac{(\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v)}{a_{0}}.

Then, using (
5.16
) we observe

(5.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{1}-pf_{2}&=f_{1}-\big(p(0)+q_{0}\big)\big(a_{0}{\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v){\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v){\varphi_{1}}+g\big)\\
&=f_{1}-p(0)f_{2}-a_{0}q_{0}{\varphi_{0}}+q_{0}\big((\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v){\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v){\varphi_{1}}\big)+q_{0}g\\
&=f_{1}-p(0)f_{2}-a_{0}q_{0}{\varphi_{0}}-q_{1}((\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v){\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v){\varphi_{1}})-g_{1}.\end{split}

where

\displaystyle q_{0}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq p(z)-p(0),\qquad q_{1}(z)\coloneqq\nabla_{v}p(0)\cdot v,

\displaystyle g_{1}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(q_{0}(z)-q_{1}(z))((\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v){\varphi_{0}}+(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v){\varphi_{1}})+q_{0}(z)g(z).

Thus we have

(5.18)

\displaystyle|g_{1}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq c|z|^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})}\right),

i.e.
g_{1}
satisfies an error estimate of one order higher than
g
. To prove it, we have also used
[
38
, Lemma 5.3]
, which is closely related to
subsection 5.1
and yields after Hölder interpolation (see
subsection 2.1
)

\displaystyle[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{3}{2}}(H_{1})}\leq c\|F_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+c[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})}\leq c\|f_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+c[F_{i}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})}.

Now, note that by construction,

\displaystyle p(0)=a,\quad\nabla_{v}p(0)=\frac{\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)}{a_{0}},\quad q_{0}(z)=\frac{\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v}{a_{0}}+\frac{v^{\mathrm{t}}Qv}{a_{0}}+\frac{\partial_{t}p_{0}}{a_{0}}t,\quad q_{1}(z)=\frac{\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v}{a_{0}},

and thus

\displaystyle q_{1}(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v)+q

\displaystyle=p_{1},

\displaystyle a_{0}q_{0}+q_{1}(\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v+v^{\mathrm{t}}Qv

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{t}p_{0}t+(\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v)\frac{\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v}{a_{0}}=\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)\cdot v+v^{\mathrm{t}}\nabla_{v}^{2}p_{0}v+\partial_{t}p_{0}t=p_{0},

where we used in the last step that
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{1+k}
is equal to its Taylor expansion around zero and satisfies
p_{0}(0)=0
.
Hence, by (
5.17
), we deduce

(5.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{1}&-pf_{2}-q{\varphi_{1}}-b_{0}{\varphi_{2}}-b_{1}{\varphi_{3}}\\
&=f_{1}-af_{2}-(a_{0}q_{0}+q_{1}(\mathbf{a}_{1}\cdot v))\varphi_{0}-(q_{1}(\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdot v)+q)\varphi_{1}-b_{0}\varphi_{2}-b_{1}\varphi_{3}-g_{1}\\
&=f_{1}-af_{2}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}-g_{1}.\end{split}

Thus, combining (
5.15
) and (
5.18
) leads to the desired estimate (
5.14
).

Hence, it remains to show (
5.15
). We are going to prove it by a contradiction-compactness argument. The proof somewhat follows the scheme of
[
38
, Proposition 5.1]
, but is more complicated since the blow-up sequence involves two solutions at the same time. We will divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1. Constructing the blow-up sequence.
We assume that there are sequences
(A_{l})_{l}
,
(B_{l})_{l}
,
(F_{i,l})_{l}
, and
(f_{i,l})_{l}
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f_{i,l}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{i,l}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A_{l}\nabla_{v}f_{i,l})&=B_{l}\cdot\nabla_{v}f_{i,l}+F_{i,l}&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{1}$},\\
f_{i,l}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{1}$}\end{aligned}\right.

with

(5.20)

\displaystyle\|A_{l}\|_{C^{k+\epsilon}(H_{1})}+\|B_{l}\|_{C^{k+\epsilon-1}(H_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda,

\displaystyle\|f_{i,l}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}+[F_{i,l}]_{C^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}}(H_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq 1,

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{0}}{c_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq f_{2,l}\quad\text{in }H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}

for some
\Lambda\geq 1
,
c_{2}\geq 1
, but

(5.21)

\displaystyle\sup_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{(a,p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in\mathcal{T}_{l}}\sup_{\rho\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]}\rho^{-(\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon)}\left\|f_{1,l}-af_{2,l}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{0}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}=\infty,

where the vector spaces
\mathcal{T}_{l}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{l}\coloneqq\left\{(a,p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{k+1}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{[k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}]}\times\mathcal{P}_{[k+\epsilon-1]}\,\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}p_{0}(0)=p_{1}(0)=Dp_{1}(0)=0,\\
(A_{l}(0))_{n}\cdot\nabla_{v}p_{0}(0)=0\end{array}\right\}.

Note that
p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
, and if
k+\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}
and
k+\epsilon<1
, they are zero, respectively.

For any
\rho>0
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a minimizer
(a_{l,\rho},p_{0,l,\rho},p_{1,l,\rho},p_{2,l,\rho},p_{3,l,\rho})\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
such that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\left\|f_{1,l}-a_{l,\rho}f_{2,l}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i,l,\rho}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{2}(H_{\rho})}\leq\left\|f_{1,l}-af_{2,l}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{2}(H_{\rho})},

\displaystyle\int_{H_{\rho}}\left(f_{1,l}-a_{l,\rho}f_{2,l}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i,l,\rho}{\varphi_{i}}\right)\left(af_{2,l}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i}{\varphi_{i}}\right)\,dz=0,

for any
(a,p_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
.
Now we define

(5.23)

\displaystyle\theta(r)\coloneqq\sup_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\max_{r\leq\rho\leq\frac{1}{2}}\rho^{-(\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon)}\left\|f_{1,l}-a_{l,\rho}f_{2,l}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i,l,\rho}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}.

First, we prove
\theta(r)\to\infty
as
r\to 0
. Note from (
5.23
) that

\displaystyle\left\|(a_{l,\rho}-a_{l,2\rho})f_{2,l}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}(p_{i,l,\rho}-p_{i,l,2\rho}){\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}\leq c\theta(\rho)\rho^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}.

On the other hand, we observe from
subsection 2.2
that there is a constant
c_{2,m}
such that
|c_{2,m}|\leq c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon)
is uniformly bounded in
m
, and

(5.24)

\displaystyle\|f_{2,m}-c_{2,m}{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}\leq c\rho^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon},

which implies
f_{2,m}\leq L{\varphi_{0}}
in
H_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}
for some constant
L=L(n,\Lambda,\epsilon)
.
Then, using (
5.5
), we can deduce

(5.25)

\displaystyle|a_{l,\rho}-a_{l,2\rho}|\leq c\theta(\rho)\rho^{1+k+\epsilon},\quad|p^{\beta}_{i,l,\rho}-p^{\beta}_{i,l,2\rho}|\leq c\theta(\rho)\rho^{1+k+\epsilon-\beta}\quad\text{for each }i\in\{0,1\},

\displaystyle|p_{2,l,\rho}-p_{2,l,2\rho}|\leq c\theta(\rho)\rho^{k+\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}},\quad|p_{3,l,\rho}-p_{3,l,2\rho}|\leq c\theta(\rho)\rho^{k+\epsilon-1},

where we write
p_{i,l,\rho}=\sum\limits_{\beta}p^{\beta}_{i,l,\rho}
for some monomial
p^{\beta}_{i,l,\rho}
with kinetic degree
\beta
.
Therefore, if
\theta(r)\leq N
for some constant
N
, then as in
[
53
, Lemma 2.14]
, we have
a_{l,0}\coloneqq\lim\limits_{\rho\to 0}a_{l,\rho}
and
{p}_{i,l,0}\coloneqq\lim\limits_{\rho\to 0}p_{i,l,\rho}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|f_{1,l}-a_{l,0}f_{2,l}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i,l,0}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{\rho})}\leq c\rho^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}.

Since clearly also
(A_{l}(0))_{n}\cdot\nabla_{v}p_{0,l,0}(0)=\lim\limits_{\rho\to 0}(A_{l}(0))_{n}\cdot\nabla_{v}p_{0,l,\rho}(0)=0
, this contradicts (
5.21
). Thus, we have proved that
\theta(r)\to\infty
, as
r\to 0
.

Next, since
\theta(r)
is non-increasing, there are sequences
l_{m}
and
r_{m}
such that
r_{l_{m}}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
and

(5.26)

\displaystyle\frac{\theta(r_{l_{m}})}{2}\leq(r_{l_{m}})^{-(\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon)}\left\|f_{1,l_{m}}-a_{l_{m},r_{l_{m}}}f_{2,l_{m}}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}p_{i,l_{m},r_{l_{m}}}{\varphi_{i}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{r_{l_{m}}})}\leq\theta(r_{l_{m}}).

Henceforth, we may write
l_{m}=m
. Now, define the blow-up sequence
\widetilde{f}_{m}
by

\widetilde{f}_{m}(z)\coloneqq\frac{f_{1,m}(S_{r_{m}}z)-a_{m,r_{m}}f_{2,m}(S_{r_{m}}z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{3}p_{i,m,r_{m}}(S_{r_{m}}z){\varphi_{i}}(S_{r_{m}}z)}{r_{m}^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}

to see that

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\widetilde{f}_{m}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{f}_{m}-\mathrm{div}_{v}(\widetilde{A}_{m}\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f}_{m})&=\widetilde{B}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{v}\widetilde{f}_{m}+\widetilde{F}_{m}+\widetilde{g}_{m}&&\qquad\mbox{in ${H}_{1/r_{m}}$},\\
\widetilde{f}_{m}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap H_{1/r_{m}}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where we write

(5.27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{m}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq A_{m}(S_{r_{m}}z),\quad\widetilde{B}_{m}(z)\coloneqq r_{m}B_{m}(S_{r_{m}}z),

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{m}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{r_{m}^{\frac{1}{2}-k-\epsilon}}{\theta(r_{m})}(F_{1,m}(S_{r_{m}}z)-a_{m,r_{m}}F_{2,m}(S_{r_{m}}z)),

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{m}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sum_{i=0}^{1}{\partial_{t}(\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}})}{\widetilde{\varphi_{i}}}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}(v\cdot\nabla_{x})(\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\left(\mathrm{div}_{v}\left(\widetilde{A}_{m}\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})\right)+\widetilde{B}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\varphi_{i}}}(x_{n},v_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\varphi_{i}}(r_{m}^{3}x_{n},r_{m}v_{n})},\quad\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}(z)\coloneqq\frac{p_{i,m,r_{m}}(S_{r_{m}}z)}{r_{m}^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}.

In particular, by (
2.4
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
) and the fact that

\displaystyle v\cdot\nabla_{x}([\nabla_{v}{\widetilde{p}}_{0,m,r_{m}}(0)\cdot v]{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}})-\mathrm{div}_{v}(\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)\nabla_{v}([\nabla_{v}{\widetilde{p}}_{0,m,r_{m}}(0)\cdot v]{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}))=0,

which follows from
(A_{m}(0))_{n}\cdot\nabla_{v}p_{0,m,r_{m}}(0)=0
,
we rewrite the function
\widetilde{g}_{m}
as

(5.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=0}^{1}{\partial_{t}(\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}})}{\widetilde{\varphi_{i}}}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\left(\mathrm{div}_{v}((\widetilde{A}_{m}-\widetilde{A}_{m}(0))\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})+\widetilde{B}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{1}\left[\mathrm{div}_{v}(\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{q}_{i,m,r_{m}})\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})+\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{\varphi_{i}})\cdot\nabla_{v}(\widetilde{q}_{i,m,r_{m}})\right]

\displaystyle-3r_{m}^{2}\widetilde{q}_{1,m,r_{m}}(\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}{\varphi_{0}})(S_{r_{m}}z)+r_{m}^{2}\widetilde{p}_{2,m,r_{m}}+r_{m}^{2}m_{0}\widetilde{p}_{3,m,r_{m}}\widetilde{\varphi_{0}},

where we write
\widetilde{q}_{0,m,r_{m}}\coloneqq\widetilde{p}_{0,m,r_{m}}-\nabla_{v}\widetilde{p}_{0,m,r_{m}}(0)\cdot v
and
\widetilde{q}_{1,m,r_{m}}\coloneqq\widetilde{p}_{1,m,r_{m}}
, which gives
\widetilde{q}_{i,m,r_{m}}(0)=D\widetilde{q}_{i,m,r_{m}}(0)=0
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
.

Step 2. Uniform estimates for the blow up sequence.
Now, we are going to prove that

(5.29)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{f}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}\leq cR^{\frac{3}{2}+k+\epsilon}\quad\text{for some constant }c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,c_{2}),

\displaystyle[\widetilde{f}_{m}]_{C^{\alpha}(H_{R})}\leq c\quad\text{for some constant }c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,R,c_{2})\quad\text{and some }\alpha=\alpha(n,\Lambda)>0,

\displaystyle[\widetilde{f}_{m}]_{C^{1,\epsilon}(H_{1}(z_{0}))}\leq c\quad\text{for some constant }c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,z_{0},c_{2})\text{ whenever }H_{1}(z_{0})\Subset\{x_{n}>0\}.

First, following the same lines as in the proof of
[
53
, Lemma 2.15]
and using (
5.25
), we obtain
the first inequality in (
5.29
) and

(5.30)

\displaystyle\frac{|a_{m,r_{m}}|}{\theta(r_{m})}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{\|p_{i,m,r_{m}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}}{\theta(r_{m})}\to 0\quad\text{as }m\to\infty.

Moreover, in order to establish (
5.30
), we need to estimate the terms
\widetilde{F}_{m}
and
\widetilde{g}_{m}
.
To this end, we distinguish between two cases.

•

k=1
. First, using (
2.7
) and
A_{m}\in C^{1,\epsilon}
, we observe

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{m}=\sum_{i=0}^{1}F_{m,i,0}{\varphi_{i}}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}F_{m,i,1,1}\partial_{v_{n}}\varphi_{i}+\sum_{i=0}^{1}F_{m,i,1,2}\partial_{v_{n}}\varphi_{i}+\sum_{i=0}^{1}(F_{m,i,2,1}+F_{m,i,2,2})\partial_{v_{n},v_{n}}\varphi_{i}+r_{m}^{2}\widetilde{p}_{2,m,r_{m}},

where
F_{m,1,2,2}=0
. It holds

(5.31)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{1}[F_{m,i,0}]_{C^{\epsilon}(H_{R})}+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\|F_{m,i,1,1}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}+\sum_{i=0}^{1}[F_{m,i,1,2}]_{C^{1,\epsilon}(H_{R})}+\sum_{i=0}^{1}\|F_{m,i,2,1}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}

\displaystyle+[F_{m,0,2,2}]_{C^{2,\epsilon}(H_{R})}\leq c\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{\|p_{i,m,r_{m}}\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}}{\theta(r_{m})}

for some constant
c=c(n,R,\Lambda,\epsilon)
, as well as

(5.32)

\displaystyle F_{m,0,1,2}(0)=F_{m,1,1,2}(0)=0,\quad F_{m,0,2,2}(0)=DF_{m,0,2,2}(0)=0.

This can easily be deduce from the following explicit formulas for the functions
F_{m,i,0}
,
F_{m,i,1,1}
,
F_{m,i,1,2}
,
F_{m,i,2,1}
and
F_{m,i,2,2}
, namely

\displaystyle F_{m,i,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\partial_{t}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}+(\widetilde{A}_{m}-\widetilde{A}_{m}(0))\nabla_{v}^{2}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}+\nabla_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m}\nabla_{v}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}+B_{m}(S_{r_{m}}z)\cdot\nabla_{v}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{r_{m}^{\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{A_{m}(0)\nabla_{v}^{2}\widetilde{q}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{r_{m}^{\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}+\frac{m_{i}\widetilde{p}_{3,m,r_{m}}}{{r_{m}^{\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}},

\displaystyle F_{m,i,1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathrm{div}_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m}-\mathrm{div}_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m}(0))\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}+(\widetilde{A}_{m}-\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)-\nabla_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)\cdot(r_{m}v))\nabla_{v}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{{r_{m}^{1+\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}},

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(B_{m}(S_{r_{m}}z)-B_{m}(0))\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{{{r_{m}^{1+\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})}}}\text{ for }i\in\{0,1\},

\displaystyle F_{m,i,1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathrm{div}_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m})\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{{r_{m}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon-\alpha_{i}}\theta(r_{m})}}+\frac{B_{m}(S_{r_{m}}z)\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{{{r_{m}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon-\alpha_{i}}\theta(r_{m})}}}\text{ for }i\in\{2,3\},

\displaystyle F_{m,i,1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\mathrm{div}_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)+B_{m}(0))\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}+\nabla_{v}\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)\cdot(r_{m}v)\nabla_{v}\widetilde{p}_{i,m,r_{m}}+2\widetilde{A}_{m}(0)\nabla_{v}\widetilde{q}_{i,m,r_{m}}}{r_{m}^{1+\epsilon}\theta(r_{m})},

\displaystyle F_{m,0,2,1}
